# Fix list

Everything raised in play on 2026-10-19, in one place so nothing is lost. Each item: what you said, what is
behind it, and where it stands. Ticked when done and pushed; the commit is noted.

Status: **Open**, **Doing**, **Done** (commit), **Planned** (a bigger piece that belongs to a phase of
[MASTER_PLAN.md](MASTER_PLAN.md)).

---

## Feel and performance

| # | You said | What is behind it | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The game feels choppy though it says about 111 FPS | Found: every 30 frames the picture's brightness was read back from the graphics card for tuning, and reading anything back makes the game wait for the card to finish: a held frame of 30 to 70 ms three or four times a second. Now only read when asked (eye_measure). Long frames over 800 frames: 47 before, 2 after | Done |
| 2 | Jungle about 53 FPS: optimise trees and grass | Found a big part of it: standing near the line between two patches of ground, the eye's own small movements (head bob) took it back and forth across it, and all the grass round you (63 000 corners in a jungle) was made and sent to the card again every few frames. Now only after walking five metres. A jungle frame here is now about 7 ms; say how it runs for you -- impostor trees and instanced grass are next if it is still slow | Done (check) |
| 3 | A big pause setting out from an outpost | The takeoff cinematic shows a copy of the outpost, and it was built the moment the takeoff began: the whole outpost in one frame. Now made ready, hidden, while the ship stands there | Done |
| 4 | Two different loading screens; want one | The real loading screen now looks like the boot cards (black, what is loading low on the right, a hairline filling along the foot) and the pretend loading card that followed it is gone: loading runs straight into "made by" | Done |

## Grass, trees, ground

| # | You said | What is behind it | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| 5 | Rework the grass so it can always be seen | The tufts were much darker than the pale ground between them and thinned fast past 25 m, so the field turned to bare lawn. Now the ground under grass is a deeper, more even green, the far tufts are wider to cover for being fewer, and grass reaches 120 m instead of 90 | Done |
| 6 | Pine trees from a distance (from below) look bad | A pine was a trunk with whorls of needle cards and nothing behind them: the sky through it from below and from any way off. Each now has a solid dark cone inside the whorls, the cards its outline | Done |
| 7 | Details right next to the site, then nothing further out | Rocks, ice, crystal and basalt were laid only within 84 m of the site. They now go as far as the trees (200 m), and past that a far band of the bigger ones, a few faces each, goes on to 900 m as the far trees do | Done |
| 8 | Footprints: the rectangle of the print can be seen | The print was shaded by a normal worked out at each point, painted a colour worked out at each point, and weathered less than the ground; the ground blends its corners' normals and colours across each triangle. The print now takes them exactly as the ground is drawn, and weathers as it does. To check in play | Done (check) |
| 9 | Rain and puddles do not look real; should all the ground be wet after rain? | Yes. The ground is now soaked evenly while it rains and dries in patches after. Puddles were small, many and pale with the sky (read as snow); now a few metres across and few, only once it is soaked, on truly flat ground, growing while it rains and shrinking as it dries: dark, flat-faced, a darker rim round each | Done |

| 11 | Teleported back in water; should be able to swim out a fair way | Swimming: past about 1.35 m deep you float with your eye just over the surface and swim (shift faster, on stamina); jump rises, crouch dives; pushing into a bank you climb out onto it. Out as far as anywhere else at a site (the comms range). Acid keeps its wading limit. Still to come: a swimming pose for the body others see | Done (pose to come) |
| 12 | Footsteps in water wrong; walking-in-water sounds bad | In water the ground's step fades with depth and a recorded wading step plays, louder the deeper; past the knees a recorded splash as well. No prints left under water | Done |

| 14 | Water should have waves | The surface moves with real travelling waves now, sized by the wind and weather, and swimmers ride them (see 61) | Done |
| 15 | Underwater: a camera effect, sounds muffled; seeing clearly under the water and the edge of the map is wrong | Under water now: murk all round in the water's own colour (a few metres to see by, more the clearer the water), no sky, the surface overhead bright and wavering; everything in the world heard dull and quieter (the radio and the interface clear). | 16 | Water and lava look noisy from far away | Their ripples, rain rings, cracks and churn were drawn however small on the screen, so far off they glittered. Each now fades as it gets finer than a pixel or two; far lava is dark crust with patches of dull red glow, far water calm and reflecting | Done |

| 90 | What each world deals with, in space suits, and oxygen that is no hassle (no going back to restock) -- your pick | The suit recycles its own air and never runs out in use; it only matters where the air outside cannot be breathed and a hit has torn the suit -- it leaks until it seals itself (a hard hit, ten seconds or so) and fills again on its own; only torn again and again does it run out, and then you choke until you get under cover (a building, the shuttle, the ship). Each world by what it is: airless and thin-aired worlds (a tear leaks), toxic air (a tear also poisons), frozen worlds (the cold gets in over minutes out in it, faster in a blizzard), scorching worlds and lava (the heat, the closer the worse), and radiation in spells on airless, storm-beaten and crystal worlds (a dose builds outside, a tenth as fast under cover, and fades). Each shows on a bar by your health only while something is wrong, with what it is, and the suit says so -- Taken out again, as you asked (the suit, its breaches, air, cold, heat and radiation, its bars and what it says): to be remade and come back to later | Removed |

The host alone sends it anywhere and tells everybody when it does (and somebody joining where each one is), so it is in the same place on every machine; a guest carried in one is not pulled about by the host's corrections on the way, the two clocks being a little apart, and is put down at the same stop by both. Its power is its building's: with that out (the mission's breaker) it goes nowhere -- but its doors always open, from inside or with the call button, so nobody is ever shut in it or kept out of it where it stands. Between every two floors there are still stairs or a ladder, whatever the lift. Creatures take the stairs and ladders for now. `site_lift <n> [stop] [in]` stands you at one; `lift_call <n> <stop>` presses for a stop. (Found doing it, and fixed: a creature going round to some shooting by way of somewhere it could not get to stood there for good -- now it goes in carefully instead) | Done (try one: `test_site temperate flat none 9`, then `site_lift 0 1`) |

A room between two corridors has a door onto each, now and then; a hall always. Where rooms stand back to back, a service space is left between them, and the ducts run through it from room to room. Under the ground, fewer corridors and bigger rooms: two halls a basement for the plant and the stores. Every window has a room or a corridor behind it now. (With it: the breaker panel is put up before the furniture, so a small building's rooms full of shelving still have a wall for it; no pillar in the middle of the way in; and a creature that stalls stops cutting the corner of a doorway, which caught it on the frame.) | Done |

Seen from under it, its surface is no longer bright as if the day came down through it. | 168 | Make it so the player stands when they can when going out of water in facilities | Swimming went on until the water's depth said it could be stood in -- and a flooded building's depth is to its lowest floor, so coming up the stairs out of a flooded basement, onto a landing or the next floor a hand under the water, it was still metres deep by that and you swam on. What is under the body is what says it now: whatever is there, a stair, a landing, a rock in a lake, as deep as the water is over it. Out onto anything shallow enough you stand | Done |

| 170 | Stuff should be floating if it's light enough under water in facilities, like desks; make it as realistic as possible | In a flooded building what is light enough floats: wooden desks (their steel legs hanging under them) and tables with their benches, bench seats, pallets with what is stacked on them, empty drums, crates -- lifted off the floor, as low in the water as each sits in it, drifted a little from where it stood and tipped a little; where the water is up to the ceiling, pressed up under that. Steel and porcelain and machinery -- cabinets, shelving, lockers, generators, basins -- stay on the floor under it. What floats is still felt, so you swim into it. And on the water where there is air over it, what it carried and left lying on its top: sodden paper, flattened cardboard, bottles, bits of wood, a cup, drifted mostly into the corners | Done |

Stains are marks laid on the surface now (decals): their colour multiplied into what is drawn there, so their edges fade into whatever is under them and two over each other both darken it, evenly shaded. Only the part that glows lights up (`look_lamp ceiling` to look at one) | Done |
| 178 | (Phase 3) Structures round the buildings by type -- headframes, tanks, pipe racks; outsides that sit in the land: fences, solar arrays, antennas | What stands round a site's buildings says what the place is now (SiteStructures), each on levelled ground with nothing growing in it: at a mine, its headframe over the shaft beside the main building -- a lattice tower narrowing to a deck, braced, its two wheels at the top, backstays leaning down to the winder house behind it, the cables from the wheels into it -- and spoil heaped further out; at a refinery, a tank farm of four in a concrete bund (steps over it), a pipe rack on bents from it to the building, two levels of pipes, and the flare stack out beyond, guyed, its flame burning; at a depot, a gantry crane over a yard of stacked containers; at a relay or an observatory, dish antennas on their pedestals turned to the sky; solar arrays in rows inside a fence (posts, wire, barbed strands, its gate towards the building) at camps, labs and habitats most often; a weather station of instruments where the place watches the world. What a room is for suits its size: a server room, a control room, a lab, a medical bay, a mess, a workshop, a plant room or a warehouse is never a cell wide or under four cells (a server room 6 m by 6 m at the least) -- those are stores, offices, washrooms, bunk rooms. In a room a cell wide, beds and the like stand at its ends, and what stands out less from the wall along only one side of it, so there is a way along it. And the rooms over the ones a building must have are no longer office after office: whatever of what the place would have more of suits each room best, not already everywhere -- a lab, a workshop, a bunk room, a washroom, a store. The dishes on the roofs stood on their posts' flat tops, touching the tilted bowl at one point, daylight under one side -- on a hub now, the post up into it. | 188 | (Phase 3) Owners: think about what would be best for the gameplay and story | Every place on a planet is now run by somebody, one of the owners outposts have (universe.json "owners"), by their weight and by what each tends to run ("sites": CIRRA its labs, observatories and relays; industry its mines, refineries and depots; independents their habitats and camps), from the place's seed -- the same on every machine and every visit; the map says who ("Run by") as it does for an outpost. Who runs it shapes it: CIRRA's main building's ways out are through airlocks wherever it is (a standard of its own, so going in or getting out takes a cycle); an industrial operator's workshops, stores and warehouses have heavy doors (slow); nobody's (operator unknown) is never kept -- left long ago or burnt, nearly all its lamps dead -- with half again as much left lying about to find; industry leaves a little less. Its buildings are called what their owner calls them over their doors, functionally (OPERATIONS, STORAGE, WORKSHOP...; CIRRA's OPERATIONS HALL, CREW SERVICES...), as far as it has names, then by letter. `site_owner <cirra|independent|industrial|abandoned|off>` builds the next site run by one. It is climbed as any ladder is, walked and felt -- somewhere to get up out of reach, look down over the hall from, or be cornered on. | 193 | (Phase 3) The site systems, laid out for missions | A site now says what it has for a mission to use (SiteSystems): its terminals' benches and whether each can be reached without the keycard and away from where a creature builds; each building's power -- its lamps' circuits and its breaker panel -- and the field generator out in the land; its locked doors and where the keycard is; its ways in (which are vehicle bays, which go through airlocks); what is left to find (supplies, lockers, ammunition); and its rooms by what they are for, clean or two storeys high. | 194 | (Phase 3) Underground: bunkers | Now and then the main building of a mine (about a third of them), a comms relay or a research lab is a bunker: mostly under the ground -- two or three floors down there under the whole of it, reached by its stairs, ladders and lift -- with only an entrance block a storey high standing over one side of it, its ways in all facing open ground. Over the rest, the bunker's roof is the ground: flush with it, a kerb round its edge, its vents, stacks and plant standing on it, the land a little under it so the two never meet in one face; no parapet, no trim or windows on the walls under the ground, the entrance block's trim standing on the roof, not sunk into it; the worn path to its door broken where it crosses the roof. | 202 | All doors: when shadows move on them they look weird | A lamp's shadows are kept (drawn once, and again only when something near it moves), and a door is in them -- so swinging, its lamps were drawn again every frame with it in them, but each such drawing is used the frame after, and the door was lit against where it had been a frame before: it shadowed itself, in patches and edges that slid over it as it swung. A door is left out of the lamps' kept shadows while it moves now (the torch's and the sun's, drawn every frame, still have it); once it is still, its lamps are drawn once more with it in them where it has come to rest | Done (say if any door still looks wrong) |
| 203 | Swim sounds: recordings in place of the synthesised ones | Going in, coming up, strokes under the water and the murmur with your head under are recordings now (CC0, opengameart.org, credited in CREDITS.md): bigger splashes for going in, smaller ones for coming up, single bubbles taken down for a stroke under, a bubble loop taken down low for the murmur. Their synth designs are out of Assets/Data/Sounds | Done (say if any sounds wrong) |
| 204 | Phase 3: buildings dug into hillsides | On hilly worlds (rolling, mountainous, canyons, glacial, karst, highlands, mesas, badlands, cratered), now and then a building is set into the slope: the land rises behind it and round its back corners, earth banked against its back wall over the ground floor (and up the first floor's foot where there is one), partway along the walls either side and then falling away, gentler further out. Drawn as the ground is and walkable; no windows under it; only on a side with no door, none beside it within reach of the bank, and nothing else of the site where the land rises. Two at most to a site, never on stilts | Done |
| 205 | Phase 3: snow-buried outsides | On a frozen world, a building left to the cold (abandoned or burnt) is snowed under: drifts up its walls to its ground-floor windows and over, up to three metres deep on the side the wind blows away from, half that on the others, out over metres, round its corners; you can walk up them; a way kept down to each door; scooped out round lamp posts, containers and signs. Snow lies on every roof on a frozen world, deeper on those left to the cold, drifted against the parapets | Done |
| 206 | Phase 3: half-collapsed outsides | Now and then a building left long ago or burnt (never the main one, never a bunker) is half fallen in: a corner of it, a third to a half along each side, has come down. The ruin is drawn where the corner was: its floor slabs cracked apart; the stumps of its outside walls broken off at heights all along, highest next to what stands, now and then down to the floor; the ends of its upper floors jutting broken from the standing wall with steel out of them (none over a door); a fallen slab or two, rubble inside and spilt out past the stumps. Not on stilts; no snow drifted into it | Done |
| 207 | The half-collapsed buildings' destruction looks voxel; make it look realistic | The ruin was built of boxes: walls broken in square steps, cracks on the grid, cubes of rubble. Each is stood in for by a box the same size, its door shut, until built: once the outpost is there they are built one after another a few frames apart, the same order on every machine, their doors added to the world last and taken away with them. | 211 | Vents under water: swim through them, don't crawl (it locked me to the floor and I crawled underwater) | What says how deep the water is under the body looked down from over the water's surface -- and in a crawlspace under it found the crawlspace's own roof, and said the water there was nothing deep, so the body crawled along the bottom under the water. | 214 | Destroyed facilities: get into them through the destruction, and the destruction inside as well (walls, floors, everything) | Where a building half fallen in broke, its wall onto the ruin is no longer whole: it is broken through in ragged holes -- rounded where most came away, uneven all along, steel out of them -- one at the least on the ground floor to climb in by (into a room or a corridor, the rubble in front of it kept low, what stood in front of it inside gone), more higher up. | 216 | Phase 3B: caustics on the shallow floor | Under shallow open water the sun's light is gathered by the waves into a net of bright, wavering lines moving over the sand and rock under it -- strongest a little under the surface, gone a few metres down, only on what faces up, faded where it would be finer than a pixel | Done |

| 226 | Water worlds: the facilities underwater, not above it (get rid of the above-water facilities) | (Phase 3B) A site on an ocean world is on the sea's floor now: the sea over all of it, some sites shallow (12 to 25 m) and some deep (40 to 55 m), by the site's seed; no cliffs round it, the floor going on down past where anybody may go. Every building stands on legs 2.4 m over the floor, sealed: no doors, no bays, nothing under the ground, never half fallen in; its way in a moon pool in the room everybody comes in by -- a square hole in the floor open to the sea under the building, the sea standing in it a hand under the floor, a steel lip round it, a rail on three sides and a ladder down the fourth: swum up into from under the building, a ladder taken hold of anywhere along it from the water, and climbed out of onto the floor. Lamps on the buildings' walls light the floor round them; nothing stands about that would not be there under the sea (no lamp posts, signposts or paths, solar panels, dishes, fences, flare stacks, cranes, headframes); tanks, containers, pipes and wreckage lie on the floor. `test_site <biome> <ground> seabed` and `site_water seabed` build one anywhere. | 261 | The water is moving way too fast on oceans: it starts moving crazy fast when the weather is higher | Each wave's crests are placed by its wavenumber times how far along the sea it is, thousands of metres out from the world's middle. The wavelengths were worked out again every moment from how hard it was storming, so as the weather built or eased, that product swung round many times a second and the crests raced across the sea. The waves' lengths, and so their speeds, now come from the place's own wind alone. A storm only makes them higher and sharper, so they never race | Done |
| 262 | From the side you can see weird water rendering: you can see waves through waves, but they look wrong | The sea was drawn in one go without writing depth, so a crest further off was painted over the face of a nearer one wherever its triangles happened to come later. The open sea seen from above now draws its depth first, then its colour only on the nearest surface. No wave shows through another | Done |
| 263 | The water clips into the platform when the waves are big; make it more realistic; the landing platform needs to make sense (how big it is, etc.) | The pad was a deck 0.9 m over the sea on floats, and a storm's crests (three metres or so) washed straight through it. It is now a fixed platform: the deck 6 m over the sea on four steel legs down into the sea floor, wider collars where the sea works at them, braced between them over, at and under the water. The long ladders up its sides from the water stay. Waves pass under the deck, never through it | Done |
| 264 | Make the water crash up against things and have foam | The sea now breaks white round whatever stands up through it (the platform's legs): a ring of foam that swells as each wave comes up the leg and swirls off it, wider the bigger the waves | Done |
| 265 | The water in facilities looks really bad with the white splotches; that isn't realistic | Two things made pale blotches on still water indoors. Shore foam was drawn wherever the flood met anything (its steps, the rubble in it), as if waves washed up there. And the film of dust on the water was lit as brightly as the lamps over it. Still water under a roof now has no foam, and its dust film is thinner, sparser and stays dull and dark under a lamp | Done |
| 266 | The details inside, like the snow on walls or the black on walls, are placed too much and look like they repeat too much; weird blotchy shadows on the floors (img 30) | The water stains, soot and rime in neglected, burnt and frozen buildings were laid on about half of all walls, ceilings and floors. They are now well under half as often. Each patch was the same soft oval with a uniform tint (the soft dark ones on floors read as blotchy shadows). Each is now its own shape: lobed or ragged at several sizes, its middle off-centre, mottled rather than one flat wash | Done |
| 267 | The water looks like it has a grid texture; make it look less like that | The water's small ripples came from value noise laid on a square lattice along the world's axes, plus four long ripples at even angles. Far off, where many cells are seen at once, the lattice showed as a grid. Each noise layer is now turned off the axes (its slope taken in the world, not the noise's own grid), and there are six long ripples at angles that never line up | Done |
| 268 | Make sure the clouds match the weather | Rain or snow came out of a mostly clear sky until a spell of it was nearly a third of its strength. The sky now gathers into its weather's cover by a tenth of the spell's strength, before much falls | Done |
| 269 | For the rain it doesn't get foggy till I leave the shuttle; is this realistic? | No: the air thick with rain (or snow, dust or ash) was switched off whenever the eye was under a roof, so from the shuttle's cabin the storm outside looked clear and thickened the moment you stepped onto the ramp. From the cabin, the thick air now begins three metres out (past its walls), so the storm is seen through its windows as it is from outside. Inside buildings it stays off, as it should | Done |
| 270 | Is it realistic to have a whole bunch of puddles everywhere when it's raining this hard? | Not as many as there were: in the heaviest rain they covered about a quarter of all flat ground. Now only the lowest patches of the flat hold water, even in a downpour | Done |
| 271 | How does the weather work: when can weather show up mid mission? | (An answer, nothing to change.) Each world has its kind of weather: rain, snow, dust, ash or none. At a site it comes and goes in spells, by the campaign's clock, so everybody sees the same: a spell about every quarter of an hour, building up and easing off, harder and softer every few minutes within it. So a mission can start dry and be caught in a storm, or start in one and see it clear. The sea rises with it, the sky closes in, the ground gets wet or snowed on and dries out after | Done |
| 272 | I found a random hole in the wall on the stairs: what is this? | A window: stairwells on an outside wall were given windows like any room. A flight of stairs climbing past one left the window half under the steps, which looked like a hole beside them. No stairwell (flight, ladder or lift) gets a window now | Done |
| 273 | Some rooms in the generation don't have doors when I feel like they should, like the Quarters | A quarter of the doorways into rooms were left as open archways, decided before anyone knew what the rooms were for. Now, once the rooms have their uses, every doorway into quarters, washrooms, the medbay, offices, labs, server rooms, stores, the control room, the plant room, decompression, the pump room and wet labs has a door. Halls, mess rooms, kitchens, workshops, warehouses and dive lockers may still stand open | Done |
| 274 | There is a hard cutoff on the lights on the stairs when going down | A lamp in a stairwell is clipped to a box it lights. With flights stacked in the same well floor over floor, the box was taken from whichever flight was found last, often only this floor up. Going down the stairs you walked out of its light at a hard line where the floor had been. It is now the flight coming up to the lamp's floor and the one leaving it, together | Done |
| 275 | If the facility is underground it still can have a pipe going to it, which ends in the open | Pipes between buildings could be run to the wall of a buried part, whose top is under the ground, so they stopped in the open going into nothing. They now only meet walls that stand above the ground | Done |
| 276 | We need to make the flooding less common on basements; it's very common | A neglected building with basements near water flooded one time in four. It is now one in eight | Done |
| 277 | If there is a cave, it should be flooded as well, but the water just stops before the cave | A cave joined to a building now shares its water. Where the building's flood stands over the floor of the adit into the cave, the cave floods to the same height, and a cave flooded over that floor floods back into the building (never up onto its ground floor) | Done |
| 278 | Ladders snap you to them from too far away | A ladder could be taken hold of from 1.2 m out from its rungs (about 0.9 m from your body) and from well off to one side. Now only within arm's reach and walking more nearly straight at it (a swimmer a little further, having nothing to step up on) | Done |
| 279 | Swimming and hiding in a locker gets messed up | Getting into a locker from a swim left the body swimming in there (lying along the water, the eye down at its feet), and it came back out still swimming on dry floor. Getting into one now stands you up and ends any swim or climb. And a locker standing in water up to a hiding face can't be got into ("It's flooded in there.") | Done |
| 280 | You should be able to use items underwater | Checked: nothing stops it. What is in your hands is held while swimming (the other arm keeps swimming) and can be used, thrown or swapped in the water as on land | Done |
| 281 | The doors still act weird with the lighting when opening: it starts normal but then gets all bright, and sometimes shadows act weird on it while it's moving; underwater in the facility it didn't have the shadow till it was done moving | The lamps' shadows were drawn after the picture each frame, so one drawn again because a door was swinging was a frame late. FIX 202 got round that by leaving a moving door out of the lamps' shadows, but then nothing blocked the doorway while it swung: the far room's light came through bright past it, and its shadow came back only when it stopped. The lamps' shadows are now drawn before everything else in the frame (the renderer's view order), so a swinging door is in them where it is now: shadowed as it moves, with no sliding patches and no flood of light | Done |
| 282 | Caves need to generate whenever it makes sense, not just mines; I don't like how types of facilities can't have some things happen to them | Fewer rules of "this kind can't have that". The main building of any kind of site can now be a bunker (an entrance block over floors dug under the ground): mines most often, then relays, labs, habitats, depots, observatories, refineries and camps. Any bunker can break into a cave, not only a mine's (a mine's most often), and an ordinary main building with basements does now and then too. (Still none on the sea floor: those buildings stand up off the bottom with nothing under the ground. Nor are main buildings ever half fallen in, where the mission's business mostly is.) | Done |
| 283 | When the facility is underground, the snow (and other things) still wraps around it like it was there, and there is a very obvious line in the snow where the terrain doesn't connect | Snow drifts were heaped against every wall of a building, including the walls of its buried parts, whose tops are under the ground. That drew the bunker's outline in the snow with a hard edge round where nothing stands. Drifts now gather only against walls and corners standing above the ground | Done |
| 284 | Add the mirrors: we added the reflection we're using at the test map, but the buildings all have fake mirrors | The real reflection (the room drawn again across the mirror's plane) was only ever made for the test map's mirror wall; every mirror in a building was hazy grey metal. The buildings' mirrors are now recorded with their planes. At a site, the nearest one you are in front of and looking towards (within nine metres) has the room drawn across it each frame, so it shows the room. Only surfaces lying in that plane show that picture: other mirrors, on other walls, keep their haze rather than showing the wrong room. (One mirror at a time: each is a whole extra drawing of the world. Turned off with the reflections setting, as the test map's is.) | Done |
| 285 | On some planets where it makes sense (snow and so on), there can be tunnels connecting the facilities above ground, like insulated tubes | On a frozen or snowy world (with air to breathe), buildings next to each other are now joined, where a way can be found, by a covered way above the ground. It runs from a door of one, straight out from its wall, turning a corner or two (an L, a Z or a U, each run along the grid) and straight into a door of the other. It is an insulated tube: an eight-sided shell clad pale outside and lined inside, ringed by frames every three metres, a grated floor, a strip light along its roof every seven metres, and a square roofed junction at each turn. It is walked through like a corridor. The ground is levelled under it, boulders and wreckage in its way are cleared, it keeps off the pad, the pipes and everything else standing about, and it never takes the main building's first door (the way in from the shuttle). The doors it joins have no canopies. A test walks one. (Not yet on the sea floor: a dry tube through the sea needs the water kept out of it as the buildings' rooms are.) | Done |
| 286 | The F to focus in the navigation closes the navigation | The navigation opens by using its console, and F is the use key: pressing F to focus on something used the console again and shut the map. While the navigation is open, F now only focuses; close it with Esc or its Close button | Done |
| 287 | Moons and other worlds that can't have water shouldn't get rivers and streams | Streams (and lakes) were laid wherever a world's ground was the kind that has water, airless moons included, where any ice there would only turn to vapour. A world with no air now has no streams, lakes or sea at its sites | Done |
| 288 | Make the planets more diverse, making sense for how close they are to the sun, not just moon spam and seeing all the same stuff a lot | Worlds were already chosen to suit how warm they are at their distance from the star, but bare rock had the most weight across the widest range of warmth, so it turned up everywhere. And every gas giant had up to four moons and most rocky worlds one or two, nearly all small airless cratered rocks, so a system read as a few planets lost among moons. Now: bare and rocky worlds turn up less, and ocean, temperate, jungle, storm and crystal worlds more, each where it is warm enough for it. A gas giant has one to three moons, and only a big rocky world now and then has one. A giant's innermost moon is sometimes heated by its pull into a volcanic world far out in the cold, as Io is. (Systems are made again from the same seeds, so an existing campaign's worlds will be different. Its saved place still loads.) | Done |
| 289 | A galaxy should be able to have more than one sun, realistically; make the suns look better | About one system in five (never home) now has a second star, no brighter than the first. Close pairs (a few hundredths to a sixth of an AU apart, round each other in a few hours of the clock) have their planets going round both, farther out than the pair: two suns crossing the sky together, a few degrees apart, rising and setting together, both warming the worlds. Far pairs (tens to a couple of hundred AU) have their planets going round the first, the second a small brilliant sun of its own far off. Each is drawn as big, as bright and as coloured as it really is from where you are: a glow and a small hard disc through air, a disc with its glare in space. On the navigation map it shows beside the first star, or out past the planets. (sky_companion close/far shows one anywhere, to look at.) | Done |
| 290 | Water planets should have their own look from space | Ocean worlds were already nearly all sea, but the sea was shaded as a flat blue ground. It now gives back the sun: a hard bright core where it mirrors the sun and a broad sheen round it spread by the waves, and the sky reflected in it, brighter towards the edge of the world. That is what makes a water world read as water from orbit | Done |
| 291 | Phase 3B: spray and blown foam off the crests in a gale | In a gale over open water, the tops of the crests near you are torn off and blown downwind: a burst of white over each crest and streaks of it carried on. Placed from the waves' own sums, so it is where the crests are, the same on every machine. None in a calm; more the bigger the sea | Done |
| 292 | Phase 3B: the deep ocean | Past where anybody may go round a place on the sea floor, the floor already sloped gently away. A little further out it now reaches a shelf's edge and plunges two hundred metres and more into the dark: the deep ocean round every sea-floor site, seen falling away from the edge of where you can swim | Done |
| 293 | Phase 3B: growth under the surface | Sea-floor sites now have kelp: clumps of tall olive-brown fronds, two to seven metres up off the floor, swaying in the swell as grass sways in the wind. They grow in clearings among the buildings, never under a building, by the pad or in a doorway, and are swum through, not walked into | Done |
| 294 | Phase 3B: aquatic life (the small life; the huge organisms come with the creatures and their AI, Phase 7) | Under the sea, schools of small fish now circle about you: each school its own spot a few metres across, turning together, each fish a slim dark shape along the way it swims, silver where the light catches it. Over the floor, out in the sea (never inside a building's air), the same on every machine by the campaign's clock | Done |
| 295 | Phase 4 begun: the mission kinds -- the survey | A survey site's mission is now a survey, not the download: three to five markers are set out across the site, on the open ground among the buildings and round them (well apart, none in a building nor by the pad), each a stake with an instrument head whose lamp shows how its reading stands (blue waiting, amber while it is taken, green once taken). Press one to start its reading; it is taken while somebody stays within a couple of metres of it (eight seconds), one at a time. Once all are taken, take them back to the shuttle and leave. The objective counts them ("2 of 4 taken"), the tracker points at the reading under way or the nearest still to take, the briefing's objective screen and line say what a survey is (its spoken line still to be recorded: Briefing/Phrases/objective_survey), and the result reads SURVEY COMPLETE or NOT COMPLETED. The host decides as for everything; the readings are in the mission's state sent to everybody (network protocol 47). A test takes every reading of every survey | Done |
| 296 | Don't add wildlife yet, on land or in the water: wildlife comes with the AI | The schools of fish added for Phase 3B are taken out again. Wildlife, on land and in the water, comes with the creatures and their AI (Phase 7). Kelp stays: it is growth, not life that moves on its own | Done |
| 297 | Tunnels connecting the facilities on the sea floor too (the part left over from FIX 285) | Sea-floor buildings come into by a lockout now have a second way out, and two of them side by side, their floors level, are joined by a dry tube along the sea floor on steel legs. Each keeps a lockout of its own (moon-pool buildings are never given the extra door, so a lockout never stands beside a pool again). Inside it is dry, sealed air like the buildings' rooms: walked through, not swum. Kelp keeps clear of it. Its doors have no airlocks, the tube being their way on | Done |
| 298 | Make it so suns can be different colours (they're all pretty close to white), but realistic | The stars' colours are truer to their kinds: red dwarfs a deep orange-red, orange dwarfs orange, yellow dwarfs (like home's) near white, white stars faintly cold, blue-white stars blue. Two kinds are new, rarer: orange giants and blue giants. Their light is whitened far less on the ground and in space (as the eye adjusts only a little), and a star's disc seen from space keeps its colour round its white-hot heart | Done |
| 299 | Get rid of the grid on the planet (the navigation map) | The lines of latitude and longitude drawn over a world on the map are gone. The faint warm line between day and night stays | Done |
| 300 | Underwater facilities should have a chance to be fully flooded if they are destroyed | A sea-floor building left wrecked (abandoned or burnt) now has its hull breached about two times in five: every floor full of the sea to just under its ceilings, swum through with the torch, its lamps out. This applies whether it is come into by a lockout or a moon pool. (Before, the most the sea ever reached was its lowest floor, through its moon pool.) | Done |
| 301 | Phase 4: the mission kinds -- a station's is to restore it | A station on the ground now has its own mission, not the download: its power is always out and its life support down. Reset the station's breaker, then restart life support at its control console (20 to 32 seconds, while somebody stays in the room with it), and go back to the shuttle. Nothing is carried: what was wanted is done there. The console's prompt is "Restart", the objective shows "Restarting life support: NN%", the tracker leads to the breaker from the start, and the result reads STATION RESTORED or STATION NOT RESTORED. The briefing shows and says this kind's objective (the spoken line `Briefing/Phrases/objective_restore` is subtitled and timed until it is recorded) | Done |
| 302 | Phase 4: the mission kinds -- a signal site's is its transmitter | A signal site's mission is now to find what is transmitting and shut it down: a field case with a whip aerial stands somewhere out on the site (40 m or more from the pad, never in a building), its lamp double-flashing red with a beep each time, heard from near it. The tracker follows it. Press it to shut it down (four seconds, staying at it: the lamp goes amber, then dark), then back to the shuttle. The result reads SIGNAL SHUT DOWN or SIGNAL STILL TRANSMITTING; the briefing shows and says this kind's objective (`Briefing/Phrases/objective_signal`, subtitled until recorded). Each kind's wording -- its three briefing lines, its steps and what they show while worked, its result titles -- is data now (Assets/Data/missions.json), so a new kind is a template, not code | Done |
| 303 | Make the game more interactable for missions: the breaker should actually open and you flip switches (realistic minigames) | The breaker board of the building whose power is out is now a real board: a box on the wall with a hinged door that swings open, a red main switch and six breakers in a row, each with a lever and a lamp over it, on a labelled plate (BOARD DB-1, MAIN, ON/OFF, SUPPLY, circuits 1 to 6, ISOLATE BEFORE WORK). It is found with its main switch off and two to four breakers tripped. About half the time one of those breakers has a fault: switching it on with the main on (or throwing the main with it on) trips the main with a bang and a spark, heard a long way off. It is found by switching the breakers on one at a time, and is left off. The power is back once the main and every sound breaker are on. The building's lamps come back circuit by circuit as their breakers do (never through the faulted one). The other buildings' boards are shut, their lamps green. The board's state is sent to everybody (network protocol 48), and the sounds are shared | Done |
| 304 | (The same, for a station's restart) | A station's life support is restarted by hand at its console: three switches under the terminal's screen (1 scrubbers, 2 pumps, 3 fans), switched on in turn, each once the one before is up to pressure (pressure builds while somebody stays at the console). One switched on out of turn, or too soon, trips the lot (a bang; the screen flashes TRIPPED - START AGAIN). One switched off stops those after it. The terminal's screen is now drawn live: the three systems' gauges and what to do next. For a download it shows READY, COPYING NN% with a bar, LINK LOST (STAY AT THE TERMINAL) and COPY COMPLETE | Done |
| 305 | (The same, for a signal's transmitter) | The transmitter is a field case with a hinged lid. Lift it, then switch TRANSMIT off, then POWER off (as the label inside says). Cutting the power while it is still transmitting just bangs and changes nothing. Its beacon flashes red while it transmits (beeping only then), turns steady amber with the transmitter off, and goes dark with the power off. New switch-click sounds (World/switch) | Done |
| 306 | Make the camera go up to it instead, and don't show the interact prompt so much (the tutorial will teach it) | Pressing use on the breaker board, a station's console or the transmitter now eases the camera up to it (square on to the board, over the console's switches, looking down into the case) and frees the mouse pointer. Its door, lid and switches are clicked directly, and the part under the pointer glows faintly. There are no prompts on the switches: the only prompt left is the one to use the device. Use, Esc, a movement key or right-click steps back, and it steps back by itself a moment after there is nothing left to do there. The body stays put while up close. A torch held up to it is brought up by the face and dimmed, so it lights the whole device evenly. Only one's own camera moves: the switches are thrown through the host as everything else is. Tests pick each control from where the camera comes to | Done |
| 307 | Phase 4: the orbit scan -- a category icon and a one-line read for each landing area | Each kind of landing area now has what the scan makes of it, as data (universe.json regions: category, read): a structure (a square), a wreck (a broken cross), a signal (a dot with waves off it), survey ground (a marker's triangle), and an anomaly (a doubled ring, kept for the story to come: nothing is one yet). The map draws each area's icon on the globe, in the list and in the legend, and its panel has a SCAN section with its read ("Facility, cargo depot. Data held on site."). With better sensors it also reads the threat (low, moderate or high, by how plain the organism's signs are there); without, it says better sensors would. A world with nowhere to go down says the scan finds nothing worth going down to | Done |
| 308 | Contracts at a ship terminal: select contracts, see the ship's log, anything else needed; others see the screen from a distance | A terminal on the ops room's starboard wall, over a new desk: a big screen everybody can read from across the room. Use it and the camera comes up to it (as to a site's devices); its tabs, rows and buttons are clicked with the pointer, and the mouse wheel scrolls. Its pages: CONTRACTS (each area, what is wanted, the scan's read, what it pays; TAKE ON AND PLOT COURSE, then PLOT COURSE), LEADS (each with its note; PLOT COURSE), LOG (every entry of the ship's log, newest first) and DEBRIEF. The page, the row picked and the scroll are the host's, sent to everybody, so all see the same screen. A new contract chimes aboard and the intercom says so (its line Intercom/contract_01 to be recorded). Contracts are marked on the map (CONTRACT and what it pays on the globe and in the area list, a CONTRACT section in the area's panel, and CONTRACT over a world on the system map). Taken on, its course is plotted; back with its mission done, it is paid (credits). What contracts say and pay is data (Assets/Data/contracts.json) | Done |
| 309 | The early game is like the tutorial area: always the same, easier contracts | The first contracts are set ones in the home system (the same in every campaign), one at a time, the next as soon as the last is done: a survey on Kestrel's own world (SURVEY SITE 28, VALLEY), then data recovered from a facility on the inner world (RESEARCH FACILITY 01, HIGHLANDS), then a signal shut down further out (SIGNAL SOURCE 15, BASIN). After them, others are offered every four to eight minutes, up to three waiting at once, anywhere in the system the ship is in (and those its drive reaches), paying more the further from home. The salvage and first-upgrade steps of the early game come with those systems | Done |
| 310 | Leads and the debrief | When the shuttle has gone from a site the host writes the expedition into the ship's log: where, how it came out, who came back, the contract there paid. A mission done turns up a lead half the time (and always at the end of the set contracts): another area, with a note made from what was found ("Coordinates in the recovered data: ...", data in contracts.json), marked LEAD on the map and kept on the terminal's LEADS page until it is visited. The terminal opens on the DEBRIEF afterwards: the place, the result, what was paid, each of the crew back aboard or left behind, and the leads found | Done |
| 311 | Phase 4: on the site -- a landing brief, the tracker only where the crew knows, a ping | The arrival's title card now ends with the landing brief: the contract there and what it pays (or the first thing to do), and "No map data" where there is none; the arrival intercom line is its voice. Without a map, the tracker does not point at the terminal until somebody has been in the room with it (sent to everybody); aboard, it points at the ship's terminal while contracts wait. The ping key (middle mouse, "Point out" in the controls) marks where you look for everybody for seven seconds: a diamond in your colour with your name and how far it is, and a soft chime in everybody's helmet (UI/ping) | Done |
| 312 | (Found on the way) Positions sent over the network were cut off at 4 km, but the sites stand about 6 km out | Every position in the network's messages (shared sounds, dropped things, loose objects, creatures, pings) was squeezed into four kilometres either way of the middle -- set when the ship moved out to a kilometre and a half, before the sites moved six kilometres out -- so at a site a guest heard shared sounds and saw such things at the edge of that range. Positions now reach sixteen kilometres either way, still to about a millimetre (two bits more a figure). Network protocol 49 | Done |
| 313 | Go for more realistic gameplay: get rid of the pings | The ping (FIX 311) is gone: its key, its marks, its chime, its network message (protocol 50) and its command. Pointing something out is done by saying so | Done |
| 314 | Update player.json for swimming, and the F3 overlay | Swimming's tuning was only in the code: player.json now has a "swim" section (speed, pushed speed, up and down, how deep before swimming, the eye over the surface, the swimming eye), read and saved with the rest, and F3's Player window has a Swimming section to tune it live. The file's stride section and walk-lower were missing and are added; a key nothing read any more is gone. F3 has a new Mission window: the site and what it is, the mission as it stands (stage, power, progress, the breaker board's switches and fault, readings, what is worked by hand, whether the terminal was found), its open objectives, what is up close, and the campaign's credits, contracts, leads, set contracts and the ship terminal's page -- with buttons to finish the mission, skip the work, and have a contract offered now | Done |
| 315 | A screen on the front of the shuttle to show the stats about the site instead (of the card over the picture) | The card that came up over the picture on arriving is gone, from the arrival cinematic and from arriving without one. In its place each shuttle (the one in the hangar and the one on the pad) has a screen on the cabin side of its console: SHUTTLE NAVIGATION -- SITE DATA, the site and its world, the local time, temperature, wind and visibility, whether there is map data, and what is wanted there (the contract and what it pays, or the first thing to do); NO DESTINATION SET when none is chosen | Done |
| 316 | The lighting on the trap doors (the hangar's floor doors) needs to be fixed | The hangar's flood lamps light only a box -- the bay, down to just under the deck. Hanging open, the floor doors were out of it but for their top few centimetres: lit there, black below, a hard line between. While the doors are open the floods now light down past them; shut, the box stops at the deck again, so the doors' outsides are not lit through them | Done |
| 317 | Add sitting, so everyone needs to be sitting; remake the shuttle so everyone can see the front window clearly; I should be able to see other players (and myself) sitting | The shuttle's cabin is remade: no wall between it and the flight deck, the two seats at the controls with low backs, and two more raised on a step behind them -- four seats, all facing the windscreen, each seeing out over the ones in front. Each seat is sat in (Sit) and got up from (use or jump); who sits where is the host's, told everybody (network protocol 51). In the hangar the shuttle sets out on its own four seconds after the last of the crew sits down (its launch controls are gone); leaving a site, everybody aboard is seated as it goes. Seated, the body sits -- hips on the seat, thighs along it, feet on the floor, hands on the thighs, the head turned where the view looks, all banking and dipping with the shuttle -- seen by everybody (and by yourself, looking down), through the cinematics too. The view is the seat's: looking round as a head turns, carried with the shuttle as it flies | Done |
| 318 | Get rid of the cinematic bars | No cinematic draws bars any more; the bars' track is now a heat track: the glow of coming into an atmosphere fast, orange in from the edges of the view and flickering (for the flight down) | Done |
| 319 | Make the shuttle window transparent both ways | Glass: a new kind of surface, drawn after the world, blended over what is behind it from both sides, catching the sky and the sun as glass does and casting no shadow. The shuttle's windscreen, its corner and side windows are glass, and its skin is now open behind them, so the cockpit and whoever sits in it are seen through from outside as the outside is seen from in. (A part of any model can be made glass in its file: "glass", how much of it there is. A dev command, vehicle_rebuild_part, makes one part of a vehicle's model file again from the code, keeping the rest of the file) | Done |
| 320 | When I first enter I'm outside the skybox (all grey) and can't see the site for a while | High over a site, coming down to it, the eye was outside the box the site's sky is used in (it reached only 150 m up), so the sky was the plain grey one. On a clear day the haze reaches twice as far (to about 4.5 km), and the land round the site to the horizon is shown from anywhere over it on its own map (it was hidden past 2 km), so it is all there from the first sight of it | Done |
| 321 | 3D clouds at sites | A cloud deck drawn in three dimensions over every site with air (ray-marched in the final pass): fair-weather billows high, a low grey lid in fog, a thick grey deck in rain or snow, a towering dark one in a storm, lit by the sun and the sky there and drifting; closing over past the land's edge, so the edge of the made land is never seen from above it. The air thins with height, so from a shuttle coming down the ground is seen a long way. Its heights are measured from the site's own ground. (r.clouds turns it off) | Done |
| 322 | A lot of stuff is lagging behind (in the shuttle) | The shuttle's screen and its seats were placed before the cinematic moved the shuttle each frame, so they hung a frame behind it. They are placed after it now | Done |
| 323 | The dash looks weird | The console is now a low body with a dash sloping up from it to the windscreen's foot, facing the seats: the site screen sits in a dark bezel on it, and the status strip along its top edge is a dim line rather than a bright bar | Done |
| 324 | When sitting, third person and the free camera don't work; looking down I see where my neck should be | Seated, third person and the free camera work again (the seat's view is first person only), and the seated eye sits further forward, over the knees rather than at the back of the head | Done |

| 326 | Get rid of the orange (on the picture) when entering: make it on the outside of the ship | The glow over the picture is gone. | 327 | When flying back into the ship I'm not coming from the planet; the whole sequence is wrong | Leaving a site, the shuttle climbs on up from the pad, steeper and faster, through the sky into the heat; out of it, it is far below the ship with the planet filling everything behind and below, climbs up to it nose-up, and rises into the bay from beneath. | 333 | Flying in I saw I was going into water, then landed on land: make the place you land match the top of the planet | Places to land are now put where the globe shows what is there: ground under a place on land, sea over a place on a sea world's floor (worked out on the processor exactly as the planet's surface is drawn). | 341 | Water planets sometimes don't have as much cloud, so you can see the water from space | Two in five sea worlds now have clear skies: their cloud thin and their weather's storms few, the sea and its glint plain from orbit (from the world's own seed, so nothing else about a system changes) | Done |
| 342 | Show a picture of the planet if that is on file | On the navigation map, a world picked out shows its picture when one is on file -- in the records, or taken when the crew has been there (it says which): a small globe of it, lit from its star's side, turning as it does; "No picture on file" otherwise. | 343 | Resource gathering: a reason to go to different kinds of planets, even without contracts | Every site now has three to five deposits out on its open ground (never in a building, by the pad or on the mission's markers), of what its kind of world has: iron and copper ore, water ice, helium-3 regolith, rare earths, quartz crystal, biomass, mineral salts, sulfur -- ice on frozen worlds, sulfur and rare earths on volcanic and toxic ones, biomass on living ones, crystal on crystalline ones, helium-3 on barren rock, and so on (Assets/Data/resources.json). Each is a low outcrop of the world's rock with seams of the stuff in it: "Extract" breaks a sample out (two to four in each), set down beside it to pick up and carry (each a heavy item); worked out, its seams are gone. | 372 | Check all the landings and make them incredible: how we see the site (the shuttle) | The shuttle's run in from high up is now a long banked turn down towards the site, the land tilting under it as it rolls into the curve and out (it came straight down a line), the site and its pad seen ahead and below through the turn | Done |

| 376 | (The same) Landing at night | The shuttle has landing lights: two beams from under its nose, forward and down, on for the last few hundred metres down to the pad (and lifting off), going out a couple of seconds after it is down -- so at dusk and at night the ground it is coming down to is lit ahead of it | Done |
| 377 | My body disappears and never comes back (seated, in third person or the free camera) | Hiding the body for a cinematic also switched off its own "show body" setting, and nothing switched that back on, so every later "show it again" still said no. | 378 | The drops on the windscreen: make them like real ones | Drops on the shuttle's glass stay where they land as still beads, and one landing on another joins it. | 386 | The tunnels have gaps in them, and the wrapping on the outside looks weird | The covered ways and dry tubes between buildings: where a run meets the square room at a turn, the corners over its bevelled roof and the edge of its shell were open (the sea or the snow seen through them); each run's end is now closed round to the room. Its way is steered: on the way it was going, turning towards the place no faster than a ship turns its orbit, then straight on to it -- not a curve bent through a point that kinked as it went through it, nor a long loop out and back | Done |
| 388 | Don't planets have a haze round the outside in real life? Make it look realistic (like these photographs) | They do: the sunlight scattered in a world's thin shell of air, seen edge on. Seen from the ship's windows and on the map; none round an airless moon or rock | Done |
| 389 | When going into a planet with no atmosphere, the ship flies into it and it all just disappears and appears; there is no transition -- figure out a shader trick or something to make it seamless | Going down to a world without air, nothing hid the change from the globe the shuttle dives at to the land it comes down to, as the burning air and the haze do on a world with it. | 390 | Finish Phase 4 if it's not done already | The last of Phase 4's mission kinds: each kind of place's work the other way round where it has one. Which way a place goes is from its own seed, so the contract, the orbit scan ("Its plant shut down", "gone silent"), the briefing and the objectives all say so before anybody goes down. Each pane's "down" is straight down the glass, so runs go down every one of them; in flight the air drives drops up the windscreen and back along the side windows in short thin streaks, shedding water. The drops are round little lenses -- finer, soft-edged, a dark rim on the bigger ones and the sky's glint -- not squares; and once the glass is as full as it gets, a new drop takes the place of an old bead, so the rain is always seen landing | Done |
| 392 | When first entering the planet the 3D clouds didn't show up fast enough: I could see for a bit, then they showed up | The cloud deck is there from the first moment over the site (it was thinned away while the air burned round the shuttle, so it was drawn over the fire, and came in as the fire died); the fire now records its own distance, so the clouds no longer cover it and need not be held back | Done |

| 394 | In the shuttle it kept playing the same sound over and over while entering | The engines are now one sound, kept running for as long as they run and turned up and down with how hard they are going -- the shuttle's heard from the shuttle as it flies, the ship's through its hull coming down and going up -- made from the engine's recording, its end run into its start so it goes round without a seam. | 397 | Entering an outpost all the textures were moving with the ship | Coming down onto an outpost the land is carried up past the ship, and the patterns of its ground (laid on by where things are, not by the things themselves) stayed where they were, so they slid over the moving land. | 398 | When entering, the ship moved in a very weird way and didn't feel realistic | The way down onto a field is now a ship landing on its engines: a long shallow glide in from far out, its distance out shrinking faster than its height, so it curves down into a near-vertical settle onto the field over the last of it (it slid down one straight slope, slowing all the way). Lifting off, straight up first, then out and away | Done |
| 399 | The middle of the map didn't load in until I got close (seen from high up, a pale square round the outpost) | The ground levelled round an outpost or a site was left bare earth -- grass was only its tufts, grown round the eye -- so from high up a pale square sat in the middle of grassy land until near it. Levelled ground is grown over too now, short and trampled (about half as much as the wild ground), its roads and worn trails still bare | Done |
| 400 | The fire was only on the front window, and still doesn't look very realistic or good | Remade. The burning air is now a sheath round the whole front and the belly of the craft -- the shuttle's, and the ship's own coming down onto a field -- seen out of every window, not a cap in front of the windscreen. It is drawn as the burning air is seen from inside a craft going in belly first: a glowing bow over the nose, hottest (near white) under it, torn orange tongues streaming back and curling up round the sides, pink and violet where it thins, gaps between the tongues to see the sky through, flickering and moving back with the flow -- no starburst, no spokes meeting straight ahead. And its light comes in through the windows: the cabin or the bridge lit orange, flickering, while it burns | Done |
| 401 | The rain on the window is still moving -- not the texture, the whole thing sways back and forth | It was being bent by the wind: drawn as a cut-out picture, it was taken for a plant's leaf, and every cut-out is swayed by the wind. A cut-out can now be held still (Material::still), and the rain on the windows is | Done |
| 402 | I saw a little weird point on the planet in the middle of the picture | The burning air's shape had its tip straight ahead, and before it glowed at all -- at the very first of going in -- it already dimmed what was behind it, a dark blot in the middle of the view (and its tip, where its picture's lines all met, a dark point even while it burned). It hides only as much as it burns now, and nothing at its tip | Done |
| 403 | Should the planet haze change depending on the planet, like its colour and size? | Yes: as real worlds' do. Its colour was its air's already (blue over a world like home, ochre over a hazy one); now the air shows its colour rather than being washed white, a warm star's light tinting it only half (air scatters the blue of any star's light most). And how far it stands off the world goes by the world's size -- a small world's air held less tightly stands well off it, as Titan's and Pluto's haze do, a big rocky world's hugs it -- and a gas giant's is a deep envelope. (And the thin dark ring between a world's edge and its haze is gone) | Done |
| 404 | Rework and improve the sun, how it looks and behaves outside the ship | Out in space the sun is now seen as an eye sees a light far too bright to look at: its small hard disc (as big as the star really is from where the ship is) inside a broad soft glare that fills that part of the sky, the lens spikes off it fainter, and no stars near it -- lost in its glare, as they are to any eye or camera looking that way (they showed right up against it). (For looking at it: look_sun) | Done |
| 405 | Polish how outposts work, like the weather changing, the time of day and so on | Round an outpost the ship stands at, its weather now shows in its sky as it does at a site: its cloud cover, the three-dimensional cloud deck over it, the gloom under an overcast, the distance lost in rain or snow, the haze of a dust storm -- by its spells, as they come and go (it had rained out of a clear sky there). Its hour was already its own, going by as the campaign's clock does | Done |
| 406 | I get FPS drops at outposts, like when it's raining, or just in general | Two things cost the most there. An outpost's hundreds of lamps were all lit for every pixel round them -- more than the lighting takes, the rest left out as they came (near ones too) -- though most were far off and lit nothing near: only the lamps whose light reaches within 150 m of the eye are lit now, the nearest first (their own glow is drawn whatever). And the sun's shadow maps, a good part of every frame, were drawn at night and under a thick overcast, when the sun casts no shadow anybody could see: not now. A rainy night at Kestrel went from about 6.3 ms a frame to 5.0 here, half the draw calls | Done |
| 407 | Do we need to add items as a part of the master plan? And if you can think of any gameplay improvements, add those | Yes: the master plan has an "Items and tools" thread now, placed in its phases -- Phase 5's hand tools for the work on site (a multitool to repair, a circuit tester to diagnose, jumper cables or a power cell to route power, and a locker of them in the shuttle), Phase 7's against the creatures, Phase 8's armoury, Phase 9's suit modules and air, Phase 11B's salvage. And a list of gameplay ideas, each with its phase, for you to keep, change or drop (none built until you say): marking things for the crew, weather that turns over a mission, leaving before dark, optional goals that pay, downed before dead, carrying weight, the shuttle as a base on site, a ground scanner that is heard, crew tags that soften a wipe, comms that can fail | Done |
| 408 | Phase 5, codes: a terminal that asks for an access code | About two data terminals in five are secured now. Its screen says SECURED and asks for a four-figure access code, and a PIN pad sits on the bench beside it: twelve keys (1 to 9, a red CLR, 0 and a green ENT), pressed by hand up close as the other devices are. Pressing the terminal or the pad brings the camera over the pad with the screen in sight; each figure typed fills a box on the screen, ENT with the right code lets them in (a chime, the pad's lamp from red to green, and the screen to READY), and a wrong one buzzes and clears. The code is on a note -- a sheet of paper, ACCESS CODE and the figures written large -- left on a bench in another room of the same building, to be searched for; pressing it brings the camera over it to read. The objective tells the crew to find the code and says somebody will have written it down. Only a data terminal is ever secured (never a console); the same on every machine (its own draw from the site's seed), and what has been typed goes to everybody with the rest of the mission (protocol 53). Tests: the rules (wrong, short, clear, right, no power), the plan's code and note, the objective, the wire, and every key picked out from where the camera comes to. |
| 409 | Phase 5, repair and diagnosis: a terminal whose supply has failed, and the tools to mend it | About a terminal or a console in three now has its supply faulted: one of the four fuses in its supply box is blown, so it is dead though its building has power. The box is on the wall over its bench, beside it (blue-grey, TERMINAL SUPPLY on it). Pressing it brings the camera up to it: its cover's four screws come out one at a time with a multitool (without one, a line says the screws need it), and the cover swings open on its hinge to the fuse holders -- F1 to F4, each labelled -- and the spare fuse clipped inside the cover. A holder's carrier swings down open and shuts again by hand; shut where the blown fuse was, the spare goes in. A blown fuse looks like any other: a circuit tester tells which (held up close, it reads the part under the pointer, low in the middle of the view: a fuse's continuity, the supply live or isolated). The isolator (a red handle on a yellow plate) cuts the supply: work the holders live and each one opened or shut on a good fuse draws an arc, heard a long way. The terminal comes back once every holder is shut on a good fuse with the isolator on. The tester also diagnoses the breaker board now: held to a breaker it reads its circuit -- a short to earth, or sound -- so the faulted one is found without throwing it and its bang. Two new items, the multitool and the circuit tester (from the locker, on the benches, and one of each in the shuttle's tool case on its right-hand bench on every site); the objective learns of the fault when the terminal is found dead. The same on every machine (the plan's own draw, the box's state on the wire: protocol 54). The test map's and the lab's benches lay their items closer as there are more. Tests: the plan, the rules (no tool, live arcs, isolated work, the spare), the objective, the wire, and every screw, the isolator and every holder picked out up close. |
| 410 | Phase 5, power routing: a building whose incoming feed is cut | About four times in ten where a building's power is out, its feed is cut as well: nothing is coming into it, so its breaker board can be set right and still bring nothing back (a circuit tester on its main switch reads INCOMING 0 V, NO SUPPLY; the board's lamps stay dark). It is switched over to its standby feed at its feeder cabinet: a green steel cabinet on a concrete plinth outside the building, a few metres from one of its doors, DANGER 400 VOLTS on its door. Up close: the door swings open on its hinge to the panel inside -- A MAIN and B STANDBY, each a red handle on a yellow plate (up on, across off), a lamp over each (the main's dark, its cable cut; the standby's lit), and between them the SUPPLY changeover, a black pointer turned a step at a time from A through 0 to B, with the building's lamp over it. Turned onto the standby with the cut main still switched in, it trips straight back to 0 with a bang heard a long way (and the main switched back in on the standby likewise); with the main off and the standby on, the building is fed, and its power is back as soon as its board is set too, in either order. The tester reads each feed (A 0 V, cable fault; B 400 V) and the bus. A new objective step (the building has no incoming supply: switch it to the standby feed at its feeder cabinet, which stands outside by one of its doors). The same on every machine (its own draw from the site's seed; the cabinet's state on the wire, protocol 55). Tests: the plan, the rules both ways round and the trips, the wire, and the door, both feeds and the changeover picked out up close. |
| 411 | Is seeing the horizon like that realistic? And on the test map you can see some stuff floating in the distance | No, it was not. The sky over the horizon was drawn as the fog's colour made more than twice as bright, plus a fixed glow -- at night about five times as bright as the fog the land fades into -- so wherever the fog closed in near (that site's night mist ends at 55 m, the test map's at 90) everything past it stood as a flat black cut-out against a sky glowing behind it: the hills and buildings at the site, and on the test map the tall things far off (the last site's spires and towers, kilometres away) hanging in the sky. In fog that thick there is no sky to be seen past it: now, where the fog ends within a few hundred metres, the horizon is drawn the very colour the land fades into, and the thicker it is the more of the sky overhead is lost in it too (no stars through it) -- the land, the buildings and the sky all going into the same grey. Skies with the distance clear (over 2.5 km) are as they were. And the test map's ground, which ended in an edge a little way out with the sky under it, now goes on as plain ground out to 800 m (seen only: underfoot it ends where it did), lost in the fog well before its end. Two console commands to look into such things: env (the light and the fog as they are drawn) and far_things (what is drawn beyond a distance, by name). |
| 412 | Phase 5: clues readable at a distance; colour-blind safe markers | The access code's note is a whole sheet now (A4), ACCESS CODE and the four figures written large (6 cm), read standing at the bench a couple of metres off rather than only bent over it; looking at it close stands further back to take it all in. And a setting, Display > Colour-blind safe lamps (ui.colour_safe, off by default): the mission's lamps show what is right in blue rather than green and what is wrong in orange rather than red -- the breaker board's, the keypad's, the transmitter's, the feeder cabinet's and a survey's markers (whose waiting lamp is a dim white then, apart from the blue of one taken). Every one of those lamps already tells its state by how it lights too -- steady, pulsing, flickering, lit or dark -- so none rests on colour alone. |
| 413 | The tasks need to make sense: I restarted the life support on an abandoned, destroyed facility | Getting something running -- a station's life support, a facility's plant -- now only ever happens in a building that could still run: never one burnt out, never one half fallen in. The console goes in such a building, and the main building of a site whose mission is to get something running is never burnt out (left long ago at worst), so there always is one. Data can still be recovered from a terminal that survived anywhere. Test: across 300 sites, every console is in a building that could run. |
| 414 | "You made it out" should only happen when you get back and dock; leaving the planet I came out of it sideways and flew back to the ship weird; when you return the planet is in front of you, not where you left from | The result is shown once the shuttle has docked, timed from then -- not over the climb (nor the ship's own objective over the flight back). The way back to the ship was flown crabwise, its nose 55 degrees up while it climbed at 80, from off to one side: now it comes up from where the world is -- in orbit the ship flies nose down at it, so ahead of and below it, the way the launch dives -- its nose along its way and away from the world (behind, out of the windows), slows under the ship, turns about to face the way the ship does and rises into the bay. And docking no longer sends it straight back down: with the site done, sitting on in the shuttle after docking no longer sets it out again. |
| 415 | Change the ship to use the cutscene again when exiting and entering; the shuttle keeps no cutscene. The stairs and landing gear are missing their animations | (Superseded by 419, the old cutscenes back.) | 416 | If I look out the left window I can see the world just disappear; make the transition 100% seamless; check airless planets and moons as well | The burning air round the shuttle now wraps the whole craft, back past the cabin's last windows, and at its height closes all round with no gaps -- and it is held at its height for a second either side of the moment the world outside is changed for the site's (and back) -- so no window shows the change. Where there is no air to burn (an airless moon) or too little, the picture just before the change is kept and dissolved out of over a second and a half: the world seen from space runs into the site's ground and sky instead of being swapped. |
| 417 | When entering, the clouds take a while to show up (it looks flat grey before everything loads in) | It was not the clouds: coming out of the heat three kilometres up, the haze of the rain or snow below was laid over everything as thick as at the ground, and the sea's own haze did not thin with height at all, so the land and sea were one flat grey sheet until the shuttle was low. Rain, snow, dust and ash now hang low, under the cloud they fall from, and the sea thins with height as everything else does: from high up the site below is seen through little haze, and it closes in as the shuttle comes down into it. |
| 418 | Make the elevator look powered off if the power is off | A lift in a building whose power is out is dark now: its car's lamp, its buttons and its call buttons unlit, its floor displays and arrows blank -- and lit again the moment the power is back.
