| 419 | Go back to the old cutscene (the ship setting down at an outpost and taking off) | The original cutscenes are back, as they were before FIX 328: the ship flown down onto the outpost where it stands -- its world's ground, weather and all -- its gear coming down and its stair running out on their markers, and taking off with its stair drawn in and its gear folding up as it climbs away. Filmed, on every machine. The descent seen from aboard (the field drawn as far below as the ship was over it, the sky changing with the height, the hull shaking), the new cameras of 415 and everything only they used are gone: the field and the site dropped below the ship, and the patterns carried with things moved from where they were made. The ship's engines are heard while it is filmed. And the console's campaign_new now goes straight into the campaign it begins (it was left at the title, where Solo carried on the most recent campaign instead). |
| 420 | The top of the mirror is black; weird shadow under the dish; a box going into the facility that shouldn't exist and had no collision; the flooded basements have the underwater look though the water isn't on that floor (only the cave was flooded) | Mirror: a lamp's light is found for each thing drawn among the lamps nearest it, and a whole storey's ceiling or floor is one piece whose middle is far from any mirror, so in the reflection -- which shows what is behind the one looking, out of the camera's own light grid -- ceilings and floors went black. In a mirror the lamps are now chosen for the part of each thing round the eye. Dish: the dish up on its pedestal counted as a roof over the ground round its foot, for the light from the sky, and darkened a blot of it; things up in the air like that no longer do. Box: the housing put over a stairwell where it comes up to the top of its part of a building was put over a stair between two basements under the open ground beside it too -- its "top" there the ground -- standing on the grass against the wall, drawn and not solid; no longer. Flooding: a flooded cave's water counted for anywhere over it, height aside, so a dry basement over the cave's water was taken to be in it; now only the cave is. (And site_fitting takes how far out and the pitch, to look at a thing as somebody standing at it would.) |
| 421 | Make sure the 3D clouds disappear at the right time leaving a planet -- and should we have the 3D and the 2D clouds? So climbing out through the deck, the painted cloud now goes as the shuttle comes up through it -- above it, the deck is below and the sky overhead is clear (it was still cloudy overhead); the deck itself is there until the moment the world outside is changed for space, under the fire. Tools: the shuttle has a tool shelf now, on its cabin's left wall behind the bench -- a steel tray with a lip under a board, a yellow place painted on it for each tool -- and the multitool and the circuit tester lie on their places there, along them, on every site (still drawn from the loadout locker too). And what lies in the shuttle's cabin -- the tools, a drive left there -- is out of the weather: on a snowy site it had the snow of the landing on its tops. |
| 422 | (Found by the tests, after 421 went in with them failing) The new tool shelf flickered on the shuttle parked at an outpost | Its board, tray, lip and painted places met face to face, two surfaces in one place -- they flicker. | 423 | Rework vents so they can be in the ceiling, so you can drop down, and they go between floors (this will be useful for the AI rework) | Where a crawlspace on an upper floor runs over a room or a corridor of the floor under it, about three times in four it now drops through: a hole through the floor between, a steel frame round it on the room's ceiling, and a slatted grille lying in it, hinged along one side. Pushed open from the duct (or pulled down from under it), the grille swings down into the room, and the way is open to drop through -- a way between the floors that is not the stairs, and one the creatures will use in the AI rework (they climb walls; people only drop). The grille is opened and shut as any door is, the same on every machine. The ducts' wall-foot mouths are as they were. A console command, site_drop [n] [top], stands under one, or in the duct over it. Tests: every drop is a duct over a room or a corridor, its hole clear through the floor, its grille in it; and every door, flat ones too, fits where it is. |
| 424 | For the destruction you can see a hard line where it splits | Where a half-fallen building's roof is broken off, the broken edge's top was raised five centimetres over the roof all along the break (kept off it so the two would not flicker), so a straight step ran across the roof where the break began, catching the light. It is a hair over it now, as at the break's ends: the roof runs on into its broken edge. |
| 425 | Finish Phase 5 (optional goals), and anything else that makes the game flow better | About three missions in five now have an optional goal: a records drive (an amber drive, not the data drive) lying on a bench in another of the site's buildings -- any building on a survey or signal site. The objective panel shows it under OPTIONAL -- bring the records drive aboard the shuttle, where it is, and what it pays (150 credits, extra_reward in contracts.json) -- and says when you have it. Carried aboard (or left in the cabin) when the shuttle goes, it is paid for on top of the contract, whether the mission itself was done or not; never needed to finish. The result says the records drive came back too; the ship's debrief counts it in what was paid (and how much was for the drive), and the log has a line for it. The same on every machine (its own draw from the site's seed; whether it went with the shuttle on the wire, protocol 56). Tests: where it goes and that it is the same everywhere, that the mission ends the same with or without it, the wire. |
| 426 | (Phase 5, with 425) Weather that turns over a mission | A site's weather already came and went in spells over minutes. Now the crew is told before the worst of one comes in: a few minutes ahead (up to four), the objective panel says a storm, a blizzard, a dust storm or heavy ash fall is coming in and about how long; once it is on the site, that you will not see far. So a crew can decide whether to push on or get back to the shuttle first. Worked out from the campaign's clock, so everybody is warned at the same moment; only on worlds whose weather is ever much. The env console command logs the weather's spell and when it next turns bad. Test: every warning comes true when it said, and nothing turns bad unwarned. (Taken out again, 428.) |
| 427 | There is a glowing area next to the facility, coming from the facility being underground | Looked into at night on a site with a passage under the ground between two buildings (test map, deploy 5). The passage's lamps are kept to it: their light stops at its roof, under the ground, and none of it reaches the ground over it. What does show as a glow along the ground beside a building is the lamp over one of its doors seen from round the corner: its fitting hidden by the wall, the snow or grass it lights by the door showing past the corner as a bright strip at the building's foot. That is the door's light, as it should be. If what you saw is somewhere else, a screenshot of where would find it. A console command for such things: lamps_near [metres] logs every lit lamp near where you stand, its kind, where it is, how far it reaches and what bounds it; and the site's log line for a passage under the ground now says where it runs in the world and how deep its floor is. | Needs your eyes |
| 428 | Get rid of storm warnings | Gone: the objective panel no longer warns of the weather coming in, and what worked it out (Weather::TurnsIn) and its test are gone with it. The weather still comes and goes in its spells. |
| 429 | Moving in space doesn't feel realistic: should the background move? | The stars were painted on the glass: as the ship went round its orbit (once in sixteen minutes, its nose held down at the world) or swung round setting out, the world and the sun turned in the windows but the stars, the galaxy and the nebula stood still. They turn with the ship now -- wheeling slowly past the windows in orbit, swinging across them as it sets out, held as they were between the stars -- and on a site they lie as they do over that place, as the world turns. (Things far off do not shift as a ship moves, only as it turns: so this is the motion there really is to see.) |
| 430 | When entering, the objective at the top should go away | While the shuttle flies -- out of the bay and down, in to the site, back up and docking -- there is no objective panel over the windows; it is back once it is down (or docked). |
| 431 | Landing on a planet skips going into it; I don't like the screen fade -- make it look like we are actually going down and landing on it, the same for every world; the shuttle turns sideways coming in; I go through a grey sheet and then see the map | One flight down for every world, with no fade and no cut. Out of the bay the shuttle dives at the place it is going to on the world -- the world turning under the windows, its curve flattening as it comes up to meet it, its stars and sun turning with it -- and arrives over it exactly as the site's own sky has it: the world, the stars, the sun and the horizon where they will be there, from where the shuttle will be. Then, in the site's sky, it comes down from thirty kilometres up, over forty out, nose down and then up, straight in on the heading it dived on (it no longer swings round and banks onto the pad's own: it turns onto that hovering over the pad before it sets down); on a world with air the burning air is round it up there. The world under a site is drawn as the worlds out of the windows are, round its land to the horizon -- the same world it dived at -- as a true sphere worked out for every pixel (a facet of a moon's was hundreds of metres deep: its horizon was a line of them), sitting where the land's edge is, with the land's colour round the site and its own craters and grain kept. It is behind all of the land wherever that is (its shell had lain over the land seen from above it, until the shuttle came down through it: the grey sheet). The land is seen from high up, sharp, fading at its rim into the world it sits on, and comes up out of it in the first moment in its sky. The ship's cabin is lit from below by the world as it falls into it. |
| 432 | A large shadow moves with me as I land | The shuttle's own shadow: high over the ground the sun's shadow maps are far too coarse to hold something its size, and it fell as a great dark blot on the land under it, following it down. It casts no shadow more than 150 m up now, and casts it again as it comes in to the pad. |
| 433 | The ship's inside lingers in the cutscene (taking off from or setting down at an outpost) | Filmed standing where the ship stands, its rooms were hidden but not everything in them -- screens, fittings, cargo -- which were left hanging in the air where it had stood as it lifted away. Everything inside the ship is hidden while it is filmed there now; its outside, its gear and its stair are drawn whatever (a new mark on a thing drawn: never hidden with the place it is in). |
| 434 | Are the clouds orbiting the planet too fast in space? | Yes: a world's cloud went right round it in about five minutes. It goes round in a few hours now, as winds carry it; the storms' flicker on the night side keeps its pace. |

| 436 | I can't see the clouds in the ship takeoff cutscene | The outpost's weather -- its clouds, the deck of them, the haze -- was only put in its sky while the ship stood on the ground, and taking off it no longer was, from the first moment: the cutscene's sky was clear. The outpost's weather is in its sky through the cutscenes of taking off and setting down now. |
| 437 | Make the 2D clouds look more realistic, less pixelated | The sky's painted clouds were made of a noise laid on a square lattice and eased only to the third power, its octaves all lined up on the one lattice: their edges came out blocky, squared off along its lines. The noise is eased to the fifth power now and each octave turned off the last, and the fair-weather clouds' edges a little softer: billowing, ragged edges (and the galaxy's and the nebula's clouds out in space with them). |
| 438 | Realistic sunsets and sunrises that change with the planet | The low sun's light is the light that came through the air: what the air scatters out of it is the colour of its sky, through as much air as the light comes through (some 38 times as much at the horizon as overhead), and the thicker the air the more. Under a blue sky the low sun goes yellow, orange, then red; under a dusty ochre one (as Mars's) it goes pale and bluish; a red dwarf's sunsets deeper still; and the low sun is dimmer through it. The sky glows with that light low along the horizon on the sun's side and round the sun, strongest just before it sets and for a while after, the haze at the horizon with it, and the clouds catch it. None on a world without air. |
| 439 | The ship landing on a planet skips the part where it enters the planet | Setting down at an outpost is three cutscenes now, one after the other: coming into orbit (435), then going down into the world out of it -- a new one, ship_entry: the ship nose down, seen from behind it, falling towards the world as it swells to fill everything below, its horizon flattening and its cloud coming up at it, the hull lit orange from below by the air burning under its nose on a world with air, the shaking growing -- and then onto the field as before. (While it is filmed coming into orbit or going down, the outpost's ground sky is not put round it: it is in space.) |
| 440 | Are the 3D clouds showing in the ship cutscene? | They were not: the cloud deck, like the outpost's other weather (436), was only drawn while the ship stood on the ground. It is drawn through the outpost's cutscenes of taking off and setting down now. |
| 441 | My frames drop on the navigation map when I get closer to several planets | The map is a picture of its own over the whole screen, but the world behind it went on being drawn in full -- at an outpost four times what the map itself costs: a frame of 15 ms (65 fps) where the map alone takes about 1.5. It is not drawn while the map is open now: 4.2 ms there (240 fps). And the planets keep the graphics card's early depth test: only the world under a site, which must lie behind all its land, sets its own depth (a shader of its own for it), which had cost every planet drawn that test. |
| 442 | The light comes through the windows from inside to outside, but we have fake windows, so the light is inconsistent outside the facility | A window was a real opening only where a room, a corridor or a stairwell was behind it -- seen into, and its lamps' light out through it onto the ground; elsewhere the same window was only glass stuck on the outside of a solid wall, seen into not at all and no light from it. No more of those: every window is a real one now, and where there is nothing behind the wall to look out of, the wall is blank, as buildings' are. |
| 443 | Rework the creatures visually: they are all grey (Phase 7, the look) | Every creature was drawn in one of six greys and corpse-whites, one flat colour for each part, its skin smooth as clay. Now each has a colour family -- ember (charcoal, glowing seams), blood (crimson, black stripes), ochre (tawny, rosettes, a cream belly), abyss (blue-black, cold blue lights), bone and rust, moss, toxic (yellow-green, black spots), violet, ice, sand, iridescent, flesh, amber chitin, jungle (near-black green, orange), slate and obsidian (glossy black, red) -- and is painted as an animal is: darker over the back and paler under the belly, its markings over that (spots, stripes, rings, rosettes, a saddle, veins) lying on it as it moves. Its skin is in relief: scales, a hide's wrinkles, cracked plates, smooth wet skin by its family. Some glow, in rows of lights along their flanks, pulsing, going out as they go down. Some have horns swept back off the skull (two or four), a sail of skin along the back, tendrils hanging from the jaw; plates, spines and horns are chitin, its membranes its accent colour. A creature chosen to live somewhere carries its colours, its size and its kind of body in its seed, so every machine makes the same animal (the next step: where they live). |
| 444 | Figure out what to do with the nest -- probably get rid of it for now | Gone, all of it: a creature no longer builds a nest (the heart on a wall, the growth spreading over the walls, being shot, rotting away), nobody is wrapped up in a cocoon at one or cut out of it, and its sounds, its network messages, its commands and its tests went with it (protocol 57). What a nest was for is a creature's lair now -- where it lives, given it by the place (the next step): where it rests, where it carries somebody it has hold of now and then before it throws them down and turns on them there, away from everybody, and the only place it mends; and heard near by (the nest's low sound, now the lair's). A creature that grabs somebody still drags them off away from the others; the trait that decided which creatures do is how much it wants to carry off what it catches. The facility planner's dark room far from the way in, chosen for a nest, is chosen for a lair. |
| 445 | Creatures should feel like they were on the planet before we got there; usually no animals, so it feels empty; the giants less common; rework the signs -- they are all grey and white, make them look like the creature they came from | Nothing arrives at a site any more, a while after landing, and nothing comes back when it is killed. What lives at a site is worked out from the site and its world, the same on every machine (SiteFauna): often nothing at all -- about half of the places near home are empty, fewer further out; where something is, mostly it lives there and now and then it is only passing through. What lives there is one kind -- its colours chosen for its world (ash and embers on a volcanic one, sand and ochre in a desert, the blue-black of the deep on an ocean world), its size and its body -- one of it, or a small group of small ones; a big one alone, never in a building (it would not get through the doors). It has a lair: a cave's deepest chamber, the dark room of a building far from its way in, or somewhere out on the land; and when the crew lands it was there first -- as often as not asleep in its lair (lying low, its eyes shut, hearing a third as far; woken by a noise it hears, somebody near, being hurt, or in time), now and then at a kill, otherwise about the site. Its lair is heard near by. The signs are where it lives: round its lair (its knot a few metres off it, the rest round it) or along the way of one passing through, and none where nothing lives; in its own colours -- its skin, its markings through them in blotches, its accent at the tips, wet -- and glowing on the rhythm in its own glow. site_fauna <none|lives|passing> [tiny|small|usual|big] [building|cave|outside] [about|asleep|feeding] builds the next site with that living there. |
| 446 | Their brains act dumb a lot of the time; make them feel alive (needs) | A creature has needs now, as an animal does. Hunger builds over some ten minutes (faster on a hunt): hungry, it goes for somebody harder and prowls where people have been more; feeding sates it, and makes it sleepy. Tiredness builds over a quarter of an hour about the place (fast in a fight): tired, with nobody about, it walks home to its lair and sleeps there -- a while, rested as it sleeps -- and gets up again of itself, unless something wakes it first. What lives at a site starts as it was before anybody came: a sleeper tired out, a feeder fed and soon wanting its sleep, a prowler hungry. (Its lair had never reached its mind at all -- given it, it did not know it: it does now.) The creature inspector shows both. Tests: a tired one goes home to its lair and sleeps there, then wakes rested; a hungry one wants to hunt somebody more than one just fed. |
| 447 | Creatures of every size: a big one should be too big for the buildings (Phase 7, sizes) | The navigation now knows where the roof is low: floor under a roof lower than 2.5 m -- every doorway, room and corridor of a building -- is marked as it is built, and a body too big for a door (one chosen big, or anything over 2.7 m tall or 1.15 m wide; a little taller than a door ducks through it) is kept off it: its routes, its steps, and every place it thinks of going -- wandering, searching, flanking, going home -- are in the open or under a high roof. Hunting somebody inside, it comes as far as the door and no further. Everything else goes where it went. The creature inspector says "too big for doors". Test: a big body's route into the test map's room stops outside its wall, everywhere it wanders is under open sky, and anybody else still walks in. |
| 448 | Blind creatures shouldn't exist any more (we might bring them back later); they act dumb a lot of the time | No creature is made without eyes now: a body that would have had none has two (a pitted head has two small ones sunk among its pits), and everything only a blind one did -- hunting by ear, stopping dead to listen, feeling about where a sound was -- is gone with it. Two dumb things found looking at them: a creature searching for somebody it had lost walked to each place it thought of at a stroll, so it gave up after one or two -- far off it now hurries there, and a place it cannot get to (or is far too long getting to) it leaves for the next instead of standing at a wall; and a hunter by nature that was also curious stood watching somebody in plain view, seven metres off, for as long as it liked -- a hunter now watches only a moment, and hungry it hardly watches at all, before it goes for them (a creature curious by nature still watches, gives ground and gets bored). Tests: every creature has eyes; the hunt and search tests show what it was doing when they fail. |
| 449 | Rework "Investigate a feeling" -- it doesn't work great | That was the director's nudge: with nobody found for a while, an idle creature was sent straight to a random point near a player, as if it had a hunch -- a beeline that read as it knowing. It is their scent now: the game keeps where each player has walked over the last few minutes, and the creature is put on it where it passes nearest (somewhere they were a minute or more ago) and noses along the way they came -- low, nose down and working side to side, stopping now and then to sniff -- to where they were half a minute ago, where it goes cold; it has to find them itself from there. And looking into any noise is more like an animal now: it stops and turns to a sound before it sets off (not to a glimpse, which it has already stared at), goes to shooting at a lope and to anything else at a walk (the stealthy low), stops a few metres short where it can see the place and peers at it before going in, and, finding nothing, does not believe it: it stays uneasy about the place for half a minute or so, going about it slowly, before it wanders off. (creatures.json: nudge_distance is now scent_fresh, how fresh the end of a scent may be.) Test: put on a scent, it follows it, sniffs, gets to where it goes cold and stays uneasy there. |
| 450 | Make it easier to escape them in lockers | Two ways in which a locker gave you away. A creature searching for somebody it had lost opened any shut locker near where they vanished, on a whim, about one time in six before it had learnt anything (more as it learnt): now about one in sixteen, and only within five metres -- until it has found somebody in one, or heard locker doors, when it learns as before. And getting in made the noise of a door pulled shut wherever you did it: crouched or lying it is eased shut now, carrying under half as far (and quieter to hear). Being seen getting in, or heard slamming it, still gives you away. |
| 451 | The signs: make each one object -- I can tell it's several objects, and when it's pulsing it doesn't feel smooth | Each formation was a tube swept along each branch and fork and a swell for its knot, stuck into each other: seams where they met, and the swell running out along it broke at every one. Each is one surface now, sculpted as one shape -- the knot and every branch and fork melted into each other as one thing grown, lumped a little -- and meshed whole (the part well under the ground left out, and made simpler: 3,000 to 14,000 triangles a formation, all of them built in about a quarter of a second). How far out from the knot each point is, which the swell runs along, is blended across the joins as the shape is, so it runs on through the forks unbroken. And the swell itself had a hard edge at each end (a half sine: it started and stopped with a jolt); it rises from nothing and falls back to nothing smoothly now, broader and slower. |
| 452 | When entering the site a lot didn't load in on time (the land flat and grey, a band at its edge), it should preload; it didn't load in, you can see it below the fire (a dark square); the shuttle did a full 360 before landing; the fire goes on too long | Five things. The land round a site -- its far land, trees and scatter -- was only drawn within its own reach (fourteen kilometres) of the site's middle, and the shuttle comes in from forty-odd out: it came on partway down, out of a bare plain. It is drawn from the first moment in the site's sky now (not while the shuttle is still out in space diving at it). The site's own square of ground showed through the fire's open middle as a dark square as it came up out of the globe: the fire is held closed over everything -- over its very nose too -- for the first seven seconds now, until the land is all there. Seen past the fire from high up the land's square edge showed against the world round it on worlds with air: it fades into the globe's colour at its rim as it already did on worlds without air. The cloud deck came on all at once a few seconds after the fire, for two reasons: drawn out to sixty kilometres from the eye along the line of sight, it was further off than that coming in; and the fire, thinning away, still recorded its distance, so nothing behind it was drawn over -- now out to 150 km, and the fire records its distance only while it is thick. The turn over the pad: the heading the shuttle comes in on was chosen from nought to a whole turn, so one a few degrees the other way was unwound all the way round, hovering, before it set down; chosen from half a turn one way to half the other now. And the fire is shorter: it starts later diving out of the ship (20.5 s into the launch instead of 16.5) and is gone 10.5 s into the way down (18.4 before): about twelve seconds of it instead of twenty. |
| 453 | Update the command for spawning creatures so I can test specific kinds -- giant ones, ones in packs -- and see how they behave together | spawn_creature takes words now, in any order, as well as what it took before ([seed] [ahead [metres [turn degrees]]]): a size (tiny, small, usual, big, giant -- giant the biggest there is until the giants of their own are made), a body (four, six, two, crawler), a temperament (predator, territorial, timid, curious), a colour family by name (ember, blood, ochre, abyss, bone, moss, toxic, violet, ice, sand, iridescent, flesh, amber, jungle, slate, obsidian), pack N (up to eight of the one kind, standing together round the spot), lair (their home there, which they rest in and go back to), and how they start (asleep, hungry or tired). Each one made is described in the console. A pack is one kind -- the same size, body and colours, each its own animal -- and all of one temperament; and so is a group living at a site (it was a mix: one timid, one hunting, one keeping its ground). Example: spawn_creature pack 4 small six toxic ahead 8 lair hungry. |
| 454 | The creatures are really loud a lot of the time; they could use better sounds (first part) | Quieter: everything a creature makes -- steps, blows, calls, hurt, dying, its voice -- at a little over half what it was, its breath a little less quieted (it is heard close to, which is the point of it), and the call that brings the rest and the sound of it taking hold of somebody with them. A hunting creature growled every four to nine seconds all through a hunt; now every six to thirteen: a growl now and then is a threat, one after another is a noise. And each has its own voice: three pitches by size before, now the heavier the deeper -- a cat-sized one thin, a bear-sized one a rumble -- and every animal a little different from the next of its kind (the same on every machine). Better sounds themselves need recordings; see the question in the report. |
| 455 | The signs have no collision; in an underwater facility you shouldn't hear the storm above; the AI says it is watching the mouth of a crawlspace, towards a random rock | Signs: each formation is solid now, as it lies at rest -- walked over and round, stood on, stopping rounds, and the creatures' ways go round it (its swell is only seen). Under the sea: in a building on the sea's floor none of the weather is heard -- no rain on a roof with the sea over it, no wind, no thunder; swimming, only a far dull rumble of the thunder. The crawlspace: the navigation counts the gap under a rock out on the land as a crawlspace as it counts a vent, and a creature thinking of places worth watching went and stared at one; only a way into the vents in a building, under its ceiling, counts now. Found on the way (a test that failed once the creatures chose differently): a climber going up out of the way onto the ceiling looked for a wall to go up only within three metres of it, and moved on to somewhere else -- with no ceiling over it -- the moment it arrived, before it had climbed: it never went up. It looks out to seven metres, stays until it is up, and if it cannot get up where it went it tries elsewhere, and after a few tries hides on the floor instead. |
| 456 | There is a giant ring of clouds around the sites | The cloud deck closes over past where a site's land is made, so that coming down from high up nothing shows past the land's edge but cloud; but it closed over from the ground too, and seen low along the horizon -- out to 150 km since 452 -- that was a ring of solid cloud standing round the whole site. It closes over only seen from over the deck now; from the ground the sky's cloud carries on as it is overhead. |
| 457 | The ragdolls on planets aren't working any more: they just vanish | A creature going limp is drawn from its body as it falls -- every bone where the ragdoll has it, in the world -- and that mesh was drawn from the world's origin. A mesh whose points move is placed, for what is hidden where, by where it is drawn from; and out on a site the world's origin is in what is hidden there (the ship's and the test map's ground, kilometres away), so the body vanished the moment it went down. It is drawn from where it lies now (its middle). (And the console's creature_hurt, which lost its name to a sound's along the way, has it back.) |
| 458 | Lots of times the creatures turn their heads really fast and snappy; a creature got stuck trying to open the airlock forever, and it was too big to go inside anyway | Heads: a creature's head was eased hard towards whatever it looked at -- whipped round to the next thing -- and every few seconds jerked half a radian in a "twitch" at three times that. It turns with weight now: eased more slowly, and never faster than a head that size can swing (a big one slower than a small one), the twitches smaller and a good deal rarer. The airlock: a door it pulled open that stayed shut -- an airlock's, which opens only when the other side has cycled -- it pulled at again and again, for ever; pulled at three times and still shut, it gives that door up for half a minute, and whatever it was going through it for. And one too big for a door (447) leaves doors alone altogether: it could never have got through. |
| 459 | Giant creatures: huge, leviathan size, in the water on ocean worlds (most of the time the enemy there), and on land too; never really scared of the player -- they see them as a toy; they must see the player fairly and attack | The giants (Giant), their own kind of thing -- too big for the navigation, the doors and the ragdolls. On land a strider: 17 to 34 metres to its body, three to six long legs spread wide with their knees high over it, stepping one or two at a time (never onto a roof: its feet go between the buildings), a hull with a hump and a sagging belly, a long head hung low at its front with mandibles and a cluster of eyes, strands hanging from its belly that swing as it walks; in the sea a leviathan: 30 to 62 metres, a heavy head with its own hinged jaw full of teeth, its length in sixteen segments thickest a quarter of the way back that follow the way its head went and sway as it swims, fins, a ridge of plates down its back, a fluke. Every one its own from its seed -- size and every proportion (nothing in steps), its colours its world's (a creature's colour family, painted and marked as theirs are), rows of lights along it. Its mind runs on the host: it makes somebody out from where its eyes are (the strider out to 300 m, the leviathan in the water out to 80, both only ahead and round to the sides, through nothing solid), faster the nearer, the more they move and the more they are lit, slower crouched -- a few seconds for somebody walking in the open a hundred metres off, time to get to cover; under a roof, or out of the water from the leviathan, they are beyond it. Then it comes to look: the strider stands over them with its head lowered to them and its strands reaching down, the leviathan circles them, closer each time round -- and then plays rough: a blow seen coming (the strider's leg raised high over where they will be, held, then driven down; the leviathan lining up, then rushing in with its jaws open), where they were going to be when it was wound up, so it can be got out from under. Never afraid: shot, it turns on whoever did it and plays rougher (it does not die). Lost under a roof, it waits about the place a while and loses interest. Heard a long way off -- its steps and its call -- and its feet felt through the ground (the picture shaking near by). The same on every machine (protocol 58: the giant with the creatures). Where: on the sea's floor three sites in four have a leviathan, and nothing else; on land one site in sixteen or so near home, more further out, a strider. Commands: spawn_creature giant|leviathan [ahead metres]; giant_mind; site_fauna giant. |
| 460 | The fire when entering with the shuttle needs a visual rework: it's doing weird stuff in the middle of it | It was a picture laid round a sheath over the shuttle's nose, and every line round the sheath met at its tip, straight ahead: a dark eye in the middle of the view with a white ring round it, and seams fanning out from it. It is drawn over the picture now (the final pass), only where the view looks out past the cabin: a boiling glow over the whole of what is ahead, its noise worked in the picture itself so nothing meets anywhere, broad tongues of it carried out from the way the shuttle flies, thickest under it and round the edges and thin straight ahead, pink and violet where it is thin, deep orange, near white only where hottest -- a haze the world is seen through between the tongues, flickering. Held closed over everything while the land comes up out of the globe, as before. And its light through the windows into the cabin a third of what it was (it had lit the cabin near white). |
| 461 | Far away renders weird when entering: I can't see everything and the horizon changes colour; above the clouds no map for a while, then the water and everything loaded in out of the blue; the land had a very hard straight cutoff against the water | Coming down a few kilometres up, the haze was laid on as if looking along the ground -- all of it within four kilometres of the eye -- so the whole land below was one flat sheet of the fog's colour, square-edged against the world round it, until the shuttle was low enough to see through it: high up now the haze goes out with the height (looking down is through far less of it). The land's rim fades away into the world it sits on -- left out a little more and more, so the world drawn behind it shows there as it is -- rather than into a colour meant to match it (which never quite did, so its square edge showed); its lakes and sea fade the same way (their square sheet was the dark square seen from high up); fully from 900 m up, the land coming back below that. The cloud deck, seen from over it, thinned away past the land instead of closing over solid (nothing of the world showed round the site until the shuttle was down through it, then everything at once). The world's land-coloured patch round the site no longer paints over its sea (an ocean world's sea round its site was a brown plain from up there). And a coast is not a ruler line any more: its shore wanders a little near the site and a great deal along the coast further off -- bays and headlands. |
| 462 | We are getting so close to the planets there is no depth to them and you can tell the lower texture quality; stuff at the side of the screen has purple round it; in the arrival cutscene the planet's name comes up too early | Orbit: the ship went round a world 1.6 of its radii out (a gas giant 1.35): the world filled the windscreen, flat, its picture stretched over the whole of it. It goes round 2.3 radii out now (a gas giant 1.8), its nose pitched further down to keep it in the windscreen: the whole world in view, round, its rim and the dark round it, its picture half as stretched. The purple: a lens's colour fringe towards the edges of the picture -- red and violet drawn a little apart -- which outlined anything at the sides in purple; gone. The name: on at 6.9 s of the 14, while the ship was still coming in out of the dark; at 10.2 s now, once it has settled into its orbit. |
| 463 | Entering a moon (airless) -- it showed an ocean world, and it's still not seamless | Looked at going down to LV-459's Survey Site 18: the dive and the way down show the moon itself, cratered and grey, all the way (the ocean world was from an earlier build). What was not seamless: the land, coming up out of the globe, was a darker disc on it -- the globe painted round the site in a colour meant to be its land's, worked out from the world's two ground colours (with air, from its haze), not this place's. It is worked out from the site's own ground now (a little of the haze over it from up there, with air), and high up the land comes round to that colour on its way out to its rim, where it fades away into the globe: its middle as it is, its edge the globe's. |
| 464 | The giant's legs get way too far back and don't move up in time; its tentacles get flung around way too much and all look too similar; the body should react when stepping; make the giants pick you up instead of just stomping on you; they can be bigger; fully procedural, looking like the other creatures, endless variation; they shouldn't clip; the leviathan: sound effects, a flat tail moved left and right, its lights look inverted, its mouth and eyes aren't visible, it should be easier to see in the water (and the water in general), it should be able to surface | The strider: its steps quicker the faster it goes and about half its feet up at once (never two side by side), each put down a step ahead of where it is going, and one left far behind always steps; its weight shows -- its body sinks a little under each foot put down and springs back, and leans off the feet it has up. Its strands are damped (they swung wildly) and each is its own: long or short, thick or thin, stiff or limp, some ending in a bulb or a light. It no longer stamps: it reaches down a leg and its strands and takes somebody up, lifts them to its head and turns them over there -- and after a while throws them away, or (rougher, shot at) bites; jump struggles free, and enough shooting makes it drop them; held, the same on every machine. It walks round a building its body would not clear. Bigger: most twenty-odd metres tall, now and then one twice that; three to eight legs, spurred or not; its body long or round, humped or not, spined or plated; its head long or blunt, two or four mandibles or none, crested; eyes in a cluster on it. The leviathan: 28 to 80 metres in 12 to 22 segments, head long or blunt, one to three pairs of fins, a ridge of plates as high as its own; its tail fin stands upright (it swims side to side), the segments turning with the sway; its lights and eyes were drawn inside out -- only their far halves, crescents -- and sunk in its skin: round, on its skin and far brighter now, seen through the murk; its mouth opens dark red inside, rows of pale teeth; it surfaces now and then, its back out of the water; it is heard -- a long low call through the water every half minute or so; it keeps clear of the buildings. The open sea is clearer to see through (9 to 25 metres, from 5 to 11). |
| 465 | If they are in a pack they should most of the time all be the same pretty much; I don't like the creatures with the head like this (a long snout); I want them to try to be like humans -- walk on their back legs | Packs: a group living at a site (and spawn_creature pack) is one animal over again four times in five -- the same body, each a little bigger or smaller (within 4%) and marked its own way -- and a mixed group of its kind the fifth time. (A chosen creature's body comes from its seed less its three "which one of the pack" bits.) The long snout is gone from the heads drawn (its share to the others), and the jaw tendrils rarer (one in twelve, fewer and shorter). And most now stand up on two legs -- the likeliest body (44%), then the crawler on its hands and feet like a person (26%), four legs (18%), six (12%). |
| 466 | They clip through walls sometimes -- make it so this can't happen; they break in the airlocks a lot | Walls: the walkable floor is kept a person's width off the walls, and a creature wider than that walked with its flanks through them. After every step its body is felt for round its middle -- out to its own width at its sides -- and moved off any wall it would be in, along the floor (only walls along its flanks: a stair's risers ahead of it pushed it back down every flight; its head and rump are kept out of what is ahead and behind by its rig, as before). Airlocks: a creature at a locked door throws itself at it until it breaks -- and an airlock's door, shut while the other side cycles, was broken open with the airlock with it. An airlock's doors are never broken down now: it pulls at one, and given up on it if it stays shut (458). |
| 467 | Creatures need to swim -- some can and some can't | Each creature swims or does not, by its kind of body and its own luck (from its seed: a four-legged one three times in five, a six-legged one half the time, one on two legs one time in three, a crawler one in four; a pack all alike). One that swims goes into water deeper than half its height -- the sea, a lake, a flooded basement -- slower, floated up to the surface with its head out; one that does not goes no further in than that, and stays at the water's edge. The same on every machine; the creature inspector says "swims". |
| 468 | Stuff in the underwater facilities isn't floating | A room flooded over the head kept its litter lying flat on its floor, as if dry, and none of what floats on a shallower flood. Under a flood over a man's height its papers hang in the water now, at every depth, tipped every way and drifted from where they fell. (Partly flooded rooms already had papers, cardboard and the like floating on the water.) |
| 469 | Only in the oceanic underwater facilities it didn't seem to be floating | On the sea's floor a breached building fills every storey under its water to the roof, and 468 floated the papers only on the storey the water's surface stands in: the storeys under it, the fullest, kept theirs on the floor. Every storey under the water now. |
| 470 | Update the ai_brain visual and make it work with giants and leviathans | The creature brain window showed only the ordinary creatures: a giant or a leviathan at a site was not in it at all, and with nothing else there the window said no creatures. It shows the giant first now, with or without other creatures: what it is (its height, legs or length, strands, colours), what it is doing and after whom, how far it is from you, whether it is holding someone, how roughly it is playing and how well it has made out each player -- with buttons to hurt it, make it drop whoever it holds, and go to it. (And the test command that asks for a giant at the next site, site_fauna, did nothing on the plain test map, with no world chosen for it: the site's look stopped before it was read. It is read first now.) |
| 471 | I want it so they try to be like humans, like walk on their back legs | Only the two-legged ones stood on two, and those carried their bodies flat out like a running bird's. Most crawlers -- the gaunt, person-shaped ones on all fours -- now stand right up on their back legs, as a person does, whenever they are walking, standing, looking about or reaching for something: the body tipped up about the hips, the head kept level, the long arms hanging from the shoulders and swinging with the stride, knuckles just off the floor where they are longer than it is tall. They drop to all fours to run, creep, squeeze along a crawlspace, climb, leap, gather themselves to strike, bite or eat, and stand again after. Every two-legged one carries its body more upright too, hunched like a person rather than flat. (Each creature's own: about seven crawlers in ten stand; the brain window's description says "stands up like a person".) |
| 472 | The sun doesn't seem to hit the 3D clouds correctly, and the clouds look weird far away, like they are noising | The sun had a third of the sky's weight in the cloud and came through hardly any of it, so every cloud was the same dull blue-grey -- from above a flat slate slab, its tops no brighter in the sun than its sides out of it. And the deck was as thick all the way up, one even layer rather than clouds. Now the sun lights it at full strength, through far less of it: sunlit tops white, shaded sides and undersides grey, the thin edges facing away darker. The higher in the deck, the more it takes to make cloud there, so it heaps up into separate billows with rounded tops over flat undersides, with sky (or, from above, the land) between them. Far off it was speckled because each pixel's few samples were a kilometre apart, landing in and out of billows that size: it is now marched no more than fourteen kilometres (each step under six hundred metres), the finest detail left out where the steps are long, and beyond that filled in with the deck's average cloud in its average light. (And a test command, site_deck, puts a deck over any site, the bare test map too, whatever its weather.) |
| 473 | Fell through the ship's floor after takeoff | At an outpost everybody stands outside by the ship's stair, and a course set while somebody was still out there -- a crewmate not yet in -- took the ship up with them left on the pad: when the pad was gone they fell forty metres through the sky before being put back aboard. Anybody still outside as the ship lifts off is taken aboard now, into the briefing room. And the same rescue for falling out of the world "put back" a passenger in the shuttle every frame while it dropped out of the ship's bay, over six hundred times in one launch: it leaves anybody in a seat alone now. |
| 474 | The leviathan clips through things -- it swam through the pad's posts | A giant steered round the site's buildings and nothing else: the floating pad on the sea, the posts it is moored on and the shuttle standing on it were not in what it felt for ahead, so a leviathan going for somebody on the far side swam straight through them. It steers round the landing pad (and the shuttle on it), tanks, masts, containers and the field generator now, as round the buildings. (A test: shot by somebody swimming on the far side of a pad, it hunts them down past the pad and never into it.) |
| 475 | The planets and sun and all of that in space vanish when going into it; make it more seamless | Coming down into a world's sky, the sky was drawn as it is from the ground the moment the shuttle was in it -- the full daylight blue (or the weather's grey) at thirty kilometres up -- which washed out the stars, the sun's disc and the other worlds that had been there a moment before in space. The sky knows how much of the air is still over the eye now: high up on the way down (and on the way back up) the sky overhead is dark, nearly space, the stars and the worlds in it showing, the weather's haze under the eye rather than in front of the sky, and it turns into the world's own day only as the shuttle comes down through the air. (And the cloud deck seen from above had a hard line across it where the part worked out cloud by cloud met the far part filled in as an average: the far part is in the light the near part was found in now, the one running into the other.) |
| 476 | When you die it didn't count it as a failed contract; we need the mission failed cutscene | A contract could only be offered, taken on or done: everybody lost at its site left it taken on, as if nothing had happened. Lost there, it fails now -- over, unpaid, marked FAILED on the ship's terminal and in the log (one of the set early ones is offered again, so they still run in order). Coming back up alive without it done does not fail it: it can be gone back for. And the failure is seen: where you fell, then the shuttle shutting up and lifting off the pad without you, up into the sky (site_lost), then home to the ship on its own, into the hangar (ship_return_empty). The return was there before but never shown: it was started without taking the camera. (And somebody killed sitting in the shuttle kept their seat: back aboard after it, they were still sitting, and the shuttle set straight out again with them.) |
| 477 | The vent covers are too small for the vents now | The meshes are kept one to a name, and a mesh made again under the same name replaces the last everywhere it is drawn: the ceiling vents' smaller grille, made after the wall vents' under the same name, was drawn in every wall vent's frame -- three quarters its size, the crawlspace showing round it. Each size has its own name now (the doors too, named by their size as well as their kind, which had the same trap waiting). |
| 478 | The AI doesn't react to being shot a lot of the time; it should not heal; no "lair", but sleeping anywhere; doors should stop the AI longer; health and damage by size, not crazy easy | Shot by somebody it had not noticed yet, a creature had nobody to hold it against: the harm, the provocation and where the shot came from went nowhere, and it carried on as it was. Being shot now makes the shooter known to it, wherever the shot came from. Nothing mends a creature any more -- not going to ground, not eating -- and there are no lairs: where a creature is found is only where it is found. Tired, with nothing about, it finds somewhere out of the way close by and curls up to sleep, wherever it is. A shut door holds one a good second or three while it works at it (longer without hands), and a locked one takes eight to eighteen blows, a second and more apart, to break -- a heavy one sooner. Health and bite go by size all the way down now: a tiny one about 300 health (some fifteen rounds) and a nip, a big one up to 5000 and a mauling; a tiny one had had as much health as a middling one. (The den's sound near where they were found went with the lairs.) |
| 479 | The crawlers should only stand up sometimes, usually when they see a player; it's too much like a human, make it rarer; they stick their pelvis too far forward | Seven crawlers in ten stood up whenever they walked or stood about. Now about one in three can at all, and it does it mostly when it has somebody in sight -- drawing itself up to look at them -- and otherwise only now and then, for a few seconds, with nobody about; down on all fours again to run, creep, attack, eat or get away. (The host decides, and every machine is told: protocol 59.) And standing, its back was tipped up by the full angle on top of the slope it already had from its high shoulders, past upright: leaning back with its hips thrust out in front. The angle is where its back ends up now, seventy to eighty degrees from level: stooped forward over its hips. |
| 480 | On the starting area giants and leviathans should not spawn; the creatures should scale -- easier early on, stronger as you go further out | A giant could turn up anywhere -- a leviathan on three sea floors in four, even in the home system. None ever does there now: only where a place is a quarter of the way out or more. And what lives at a site is as dangerous as the place is far from home: near home the big ones are rarer (one in twelve, against one in five in the far reaches), a hunter is now and then something gentler -- curious, or keeping to its own ground -- and everything is three quarters as hard to kill and hits three quarters as hard; out at the far reaches it is a third again on both. |
| 481 | The planet in the sky and its ring look wrong still (img 83) | Seen up through air, a planet in the sky is lit by adding its light to the sky's, which is right by day -- but its night side added nothing, so half of a ringed planet was the sky's own colour, as if there were nothing there and the sky and the clouds showed through it. The sky behind its night side is darkened now, so the whole disc is there. Its rings were drawn as a scatter of thin faint lines; they are a sheet now, with their bands and gaps in it, and they throw their shadow across the planet's face where the line to the sun crosses them. (Checked on an airless moon -- a solid dark disc there as before; a world with air under a ringed planet not yet looked at.) |
| 482 | The signs should wrap around everything, like buildings too (img 82) | The organism's growths spread over the ground like roots and stopped dead at the foot of any wall they met, lying flat beside the building. A branch that reaches a building now climbs it: held to the wall, up it, wandering a little along the face, and over the edge of the roof and in across it -- one in one, the same surface. And one of a formation's arms, more often than not, grows towards the nearest building in its reach. (A test: one grown beside a building is up its wall well past head height. Not yet looked at in a game.) |
| 483 | The airlocks need to be a lot bigger and not stick out of the facility, to fit more people; underwater the airlock should drain even with water inside; buildings only fully flooded if there is destruction | Every airlock was a steel box built out from the building's wall, 3.4 by 3.6 metres. Where a way out opens into a room with space behind it -- three cells along the wall, two deep, none of it a doorway -- the building is now planned with that ground kept clear (no furniture, no pillars), and the airlock is built inside the building, in that room: a steel-lined chamber 5.2 by 4.9 metres, the building's door to the outside its outer door and its own door into the room its inner one (still never open together), AIRLOCK over that door. The way out is put there wherever a building has such a room by a wall; where it has none, the airlock is built out from the wall as before. On the sea's floor that chamber is the lockout. And a lockout with both its doors shut now pumps itself dry over a cycle: come in from the sea and the door shut behind you, the water goes down round you, rather than standing there until you asked for the inner door. A building on the sea's floor is only ever full of the sea to its roof where it can be seen broken open -- half fallen in, a wreck -- never one standing whole. (The interlock itself was sound: a test shows the two doors never open together.) |
| 484 | The giants and leviathans all look the same and aren't meshing correctly; lots more types, very different (not all tentacles, not all glowing, not all snake-like); the giant's tentacles stiff and broken; legs coming out of its head; popping up and down stepping over things; the grab pointless, dropping you randomly; the screen shaking a lot when picked up; grabbing through the shuttle; leviathan faces messed up, swimming differently, surfacing too often and oddly; be able to kill them | Both rebuilt. The strider is now a creature's own body -- any of the creatures' body plans, heads, tails, spines, plates and markings, strands and lights only on the few that have them -- built at a creature's size and drawn twelve to forty times as big, eighteen to sixty metres tall: one seamless skin, its legs its own (none can come out of its head), posed by the creatures' own rig run as slowly as something that size moves, its feet put down on the ground under them, never stepping up onto what is built. The leviathan is a body of its own, one seamless skin over a spine of bones, in five kinds that swim each their own way: a sea serpent waving from side to side, a whale beating its flukes up and down, a deep plated fish beating a tall tail, a ray flying on wide beating wings, a squid going mantle first with its arms trailing behind it -- each with a proper face (a mouth with teeth, eyes lit on some, dark wet glass on the rest), glowing spots only on some. It comes up to the surface only once in some minutes, easing up and down rather than bobbing. The grab has a point now and is never let go of for nothing: the strider lifts you to its eyes and turns you over -- then, hungry or angry, eats you, a bite at a time, or, played with enough, throws you away hard; the leviathan takes you in its jaws and drags you down, chewing, and lets go of you far under, to swim back up from if you can. Otherwise only struggling free, being shot enough, or killing it lets you go. Held, the picture is held steady (the shake meant for a creature carrying you, and the ground shaking under its feet, both nearly gone). It cannot reach you in the shuttle, nor through a wall or a roof. And both can be killed: a great deal of shooting, the head two and a half times as hurt; a strider topples over onto its side, a leviathan rolls belly up and floats to the surface (giant_hurt to try it). |
| 485 | Make sure bodies float in the water if it makes sense | A creature killed in water lay on the bottom like one killed on dry ground. Its body is buoyed up now -- each part under the water pushed up a little more than it weighs and slowed by the water -- so it comes up and lies at the surface, bobbing; one heavily plated sinks, slowly. (A giant's: a leviathan rolls belly up and floats to the surface: 484.) Not yet looked at in a game. |
| 486 | I want the giants to look like they did before -- the legs, the look -- not the normal creatures sized up; the leviathan's face messed up, lights floating, really big, moving its tail more than itself; make sure both their AI is done; scary features -- the leviathan turning its lights off and flanking you | The strider is its own again: a hull hung high between three to eight long jointed legs, a head low at its front, its own shape every time -- long or round, humped, sagging, in sections or one, spined or plated, mandibles, crests, horns -- and strands hanging under it on some only. What was wrong with it before is fixed: it is one mesh now (ball joints at its hips and knees, no seams), no leg ever joins it at its front where its head hangs (one came out of its head), its strands are smooth tubes that hang and swing softly instead of stiff rods coming apart at the joints, and its height is eased so it does not pop up and down stepping over things. The leviathan: as much as a hundred and forty metres long now; its swimming beat goes with how fast it is going -- a stroke for so far forward -- and smaller, so its body goes forward instead of its tail thrashing; a tidier mouth with smaller teeth; its lights only where there is body to carry them (they had hung by themselves among a squid's arms and down a ray's tail). And something frightening from each: a leviathan that has seen you puts its lights out and goes round behind you and below you in the dark, and they flare as it comes; a strider looked at from a way off stands dead still, its eyes dimmed, for as long as you look -- and comes on the moment you look away. A big leviathan rises to just under the surface when it rushes, its jaws as wide as its head (it could never reach anybody swimming near the top). |
| 487 | Sun rays can still happen even if the sun is covered -- is this realistic?; get rid of the 2D clouds and make the 3D ones interact with all the weather, like lightning; the clouds don't cover everything when they should, like on water planets | Not realistic, no. The lens's ghosts are thrown by the sun seen in the picture -- but the cloud deck is drawn over the picture afterwards, so the picture alone said the sun was out behind a sky full of cloud. They are only as bright now as the sun that comes through the cloud between it and the eye: none under an overcast. The sky's own painted clouds are gone altogether -- the deck in three dimensions is the only cloud, from the ground and from above -- and lightning now lights the deck from inside, the whole of it, deepest and towards the strike most, and the far deck with it. And seen from above coming down, an overcast deck (a sea world's grey) no longer thins away past the site's land, leaving a band of bright world between it and the horizon: it goes on to the horizon. (Dead code with them: the sky's cloud drift.) |
| 488 | The rain needs to be improved: little splashes where it lands | Rain fell and vanished into the ground. Where it lands now -- on the ground, a roof, a pad, whatever is under it out in the open, never under a roof -- a drop throws up a little splash: a ring spreading and fading and two droplets thrown up and out and falling back, gone in a fifth of a second; thick on the ground near you, as many as it is raining hard. |
| 489 | They should work together or hunt in packs: one distracts and the others sneak up behind the player, or they attack together | When two of a kind are after the same person, they work it between them now. One stalking, with the other already at them in front, goes round behind them -- the far side from the other, out of their sight -- so they are caught between the two; and the one in front, while the other is still coming round, keeps them looking at it: a roar in their face now and then, holding their eyes. Then both are on them. (A test: given another at somebody, a stalker's place to wait moves round behind them, the far side from it.) |
| 490 | Weather awareness: in a storm, go inside | Creatures paid the weather no mind. In a bad storm now -- rain, snow, dust or ash coming down hard -- one out in the open goes in under a roof, and once in, keeps in: it wanders only where there is a roof over it until the weather eases. (One too big for the doors stays out in it.) |
| 491 | Go through all the commands: get rid of the ones we don't need or that don't work any more, and fix the ones we still need | Ran nearly every one of the 275 console commands in scripted runs, on the ship, the testing area, a site, the lab and the title (only those that write files -- sound_bake, player_save, the editor's saves, model_export/import -- and the network ones were left out). Removed: two of the three `look_sun`s (each registered over the last, so only the last ever ran; the one kept now finds the sun where the sky draws it and logs the other bodies near it), `drawn_far` (`far_things` does it and gives the nearest of each), `teleport` (`goto` does it, and faces a way), `light_report` (folded into `lamps_near`, which now prints each lamp's kind, mood, circuit and how lit, as well as logging it). Fixed: `universe home` ended the game (it read "home" as a number); every command given a word where it wants a number now says so instead of crashing (the console catches what the command throws); `editor` said "open or close" and only ever opened -- in it, with no name, it now leaves for the title; `site_fitting` lacked the sea floor's suit racks, chambers and tanks; `mission_skip` and `mission_finish` did nothing without a word when there was nothing to do; `lab`'s description still spoke of the lair. Scripted runs (--exec) now copy what the console prints into the log, so a command that failed shows there. |
| 492 | Find all new sounds for the creatures, and keep the call and the warning | Every sound a creature made was a synthesised placeholder. Twelve of them are recordings now -- 52 takes from 38 CC0 recordings on freesound.org (each licence checked on its own page; listed in Docs/CREDITS.md): growls, hurt cries, death cries, bites (a bite and bone crunches), breathing, insect-like chittering and clicking, claws scrabbling as it climbs, swipes, lunging attack cries, the wet tearing of a grab, heavy footfalls (a giant's stamp and a monster's tread) and the light patter of claws. Each made mono 48 kHz, cut to a single take, faded at its ends and brought to the peak the placeholders had, so nothing in the game needed turning up or down. The call and the warning are kept as they were; their recipes are the only ones left in Assets/Data/Sounds/Creature.json, so sound_bake can never write over the recordings. The giants and leviathans use the same sounds pitched down. (`snd` now plays a sound by the name the game knows it by -- `snd Creature/growl` -- and takes a pitch.) |
| 493 | For the arriving cutscene it can be rotating the ship over the site | Coming into orbit (ship_arrive), its last shot drifted a few metres behind the ship. It goes round it now: from behind it on one side, over and round behind it to the other, slowly, the world under it in the background the whole way (a sweep across the back of it -- round the front, the world was behind the camera and there was only space), its name coming up as it goes. Three seconds longer. |
| 494 | Lots and lots of new creature variants, so they can all look very different and never feel like a repeat | Eleven new parts of a body's outline, each on a chance of its own, so they come in every combination on top of the four kinds of body, seven builds, six heads, fifteen colour families, seven patterns, horns, sails, tendrils, plates, spines, growths and glowing spots there were: how the tail ends -- a bony club with spikes round its knot, a stinger hooked up over it, a ribbed fan of skin, or a cluster of spikes; tusks curving up out of the lower jaw, or mandibles, a pincer either side of the mouth; feelers off the brow; a mane of quills down the neck and over the shoulders; spurs out of the front legs' elbows or knees; tall fins along the back where there is no sail; a swollen belly hanging under it. Each turns up on 6 to 20 bodies in a hundred; a pack is still one kind, alike. Tested: each turns up, and each makes a whole skin standing on the ground. |
| 495 | A lot of the missions are way too short and there aren't enough of them; they need to use the environment; missions in the caves | A first step, with what the missions already are (the rest of Phase 6 is on the list at the end). A signal's transmitter is now set up indoors about four times in ten -- on the floor of a room of one of the site's buildings, in front of a bench, a basement's sometimes -- so it is tracked down to a building from outside, its tracker beeping faster the nearer it is, and then hunted for through the rooms in the dark; the rest stay out on the ground. A survey sets out four to six markers instead of three to five, out to the site's edges. Where a site has a cave (a mine's), the data is down it, on its survey station in the deepest dry chamber, six times in ten instead of a third of the time. |
| 496 | Missions only on contracts (and only things you can actually do) | Every place gone down to had its work waiting, contract or not. Now, in a campaign, there is work only where there is a contract for it or a finding that led there; anywhere else the place is only looked round -- no terminal, no markers, nothing to work -- its deposits there to gather, and the shuttle free to go whenever (its objective: look round, gather what you can, and back to the shuttle when you are done; "SITE EXPLORED" on the way back). The set contracts at the start, every open contract and every finding still have their work. (Off the campaign -- test_site, the facility command -- every site still has its mission.) |
| 497 | The ratings need updating | A place's difficulty on the map and the terminal now takes in what changed: with no contract and no finding there it says "No contract: only looking round" in place of the work it would have been (FIX 496), and it is rated for the place alone; and how far from home it is counts whatever the sensors read -- "Further out: tougher things live here", "Far from home: what lives out here is stronger" -- as the creatures there are bigger and harder to kill the further out (FIX 480). |
| 498 | Update Docs/AI.md | It still described the nest -- the heart, the growth, cocoons, healing at it -- and lairs, door times and health from before the Phase 7 rework. It now opens with where the AI stands: no nest, lair or healing, sleeping anywhere, placed by the place, difficulty by distance, needs, sizes, health and damage by size, shooters known, doors, packs working together, the weather, standing up being rare, no blind ones, swimming and floating, the bodies' parts, the recorded sounds, and the giants' and leviathans' minds; the old sections are corrected to match. |
| 499 | Do full checks on the leviathans and giants once done with the AI, to make sure they work smoothly and well | Played through both on real sites. The strider: wandering, it noticed somebody out in the open 105 m off, came over, reached down and lifted them to its eyes, turned them over six seconds and -- not hungry -- threw them away, then went looking for where they landed; inside the shuttle it never sensed anybody. But it threw from its eyes, fifty metres up, and the fall always killed: the throw was only another death. It now swings them down in front of it, a few metres off the ground, before it flings them away along the ground -- hurt (20, and the landing), not killed. The leviathan: wandering, it noticed a swimmer at 21 m, put its lights out and went round behind and below them, struck, and dragged them down chewing -- but at 14 a bite everybody was dead a moment before it would have let them go far under; its bites are 10 now (more when it is angry), so a held swimmer can live to be let go and swim back up. Both draw and move smoothly; the game held 110 frames a second with the strider about. (And test_site's scratch campaign had made every test site a no-contract place (FIX 496): only the campaign's own landing site is now.) |
| 500 | The player's dead body stopped rendering on planets, glitching around like the head was teleporting | Three things. With everybody down, the film of the shuttle leaving without them (site_lost) hid every body not in the shuttle -- the dead ones lying where they fell too, so on any planet the crew was lost on the body vanished; the dead are never hidden by a cinematic now. The body's head is hidden from its own player's camera so they do not look out through it, and stayed so dead: the body lay headless, the head popping in and out of the picture; dead, it is shown. And on a site's uneven land the body never came to rest -- its limbs crept down the slope under them and jumped now and then, and it was simulated for ever; it lies still a few seconds after it falls now. |
| 501 | Get rid of these creature sounds: step_heavy_1, breath_4, bite_2, climb_1, growl_1, growl_4, all the hurt sounds (find new ones), all lunge sounds but 1 (find new ones), swipe 2 and 4, all the light steps (find new ones); find sounds for call and warning; make sure all the sounds in the game are in the audio folder | All gone, and what was left numbered again from 1. New, CC0 from freesound.org (each licence checked, Docs/CREDITS.md): five hurt cries (a dragon's hurt cry, a creature reeling from a blow, a short monster cry, a small alien's pained one), three lunges (a short monster roar, a goblin's snarl, a short dragon roar) beside the one kept, six light steps (bare padded feet and claws ticking on a hard floor), three calls (a monster's howl, a screech, a creature's warning howl) and four warnings (a xenomorph-like hiss and growl, hissing, a monster's hiss). The warning had never been played at all -- a creature warning somebody off its ground did it in silence; it hisses now. Every sound the game plays (132 names) has its files in Assets/Audio and nothing is made while it runs; the nest's ambience and an engine for a vehicle the game does not have, used by nothing, are gone. |
| 502 | Looking towards a mirror my frames dropped to 18 and it looked wrong in the mirror; viewing another planet with a planet in the way so it got close, the fps dropped to 15 | The mirror: what it shows is the world drawn a second time from a camera reflected across it -- and all of it was, every time: the whole site's land, its grass, its trees and far scenery, behind the walls of a washroom. It draws only what is in the reflected camera's view now, and, for a mirror in a building, only what is within thirty metres and none of the great outdoor meshes the room's walls hide (which were what showed wrongly in it): the pass costs about a millisecond. The map: its camera went wherever it was turned and zoomed, through or right up against a world between it and the one looked at -- the screen all of that world close up, at its costliest. It keeps clear of the system's bodies now: one in the way, it stops in front of it (eased, no jerk), so what is looked at stays in view. |
| 503 | Next to facilities that are mostly underground there is light green next to them and stuff isn't spawning above it; grass spawns in the stairwell | The land round a building was levelled -- trampled flat, nothing growing or lying on it -- over the whole rectangle the building's plan takes up, its buried floors too: over a mine's bunker, with only its entrance block standing, a great bare pale-green patch running along the block's walls. The land is still laid level over all of it (so it lies on the bunker's roof, as before), but what grows and lies about now goes by what of a building stands above the ground, cell by cell: grass, plants and litter grow over a buried part up to the walls that stand. (Rocks, crates and tanks still keep off the whole of it: one sunk into the bunker's roof would have shown through its ceiling.) Inside, the top of a stairwell up out of the buried floors had been floored as that ground -- grass on the landing; it is a floor now. |
| 504 | Doors aren't connecting all the way | A room's door is a panel 1.18 m wide in a doorway 1.2 m wide and a little lower than it, so it swings clear -- and with nothing on the frame to close against, a line of light showed down each side and across its top, seen at an angle (the outside bright through it). Each door is drawn a little bigger than it is felt now -- two centimetres past each side and one past its head, lapping into its frame as a door laps its stop -- so it meets its frame all round; it still swings and collides as before. |
| 505 | Rework the leviathans: just serpents and variations of them, nothing like a squid or a stingray; their faces are very messed up; I liked the original segmented design more -- too smooth isn't scary; if they aren't flanking they shouldn't turn their lights off; leviathans don't grab you; when dying they should ragdoll | Every leviathan is a serpent now, of one of five kinds: a sea serpent (a crest down its back on some), an eel (flattened, a fin ribbon down its back and under its tail, waving wider), an armoured serpent (thick, its rings overlapping plates ridged along its back, stiffer), a frilled one (a fan of webbed spines round its neck) and a barbed one (a barb standing up from every ring of its back). All of them segmented again -- rings swelling between dark grooves the length of the body. A new face: a long, low skull over a lower jaw, a mouth line the length of the head with a row of teeth down each lip and a fang near the front, the inside near black, the eyes high on the skull under heavy brows (the head no longer bends between two bones, which had sheared its jaws). Its lights go out only while it is actually going round behind somebody in the dark, not when it merely comes to look. It never takes anybody: it bites in passing. And dead, it is a body: its spine a chain let go, borne up by the water to lie along the surface (an armoured one sinking under its plates to lie on the floor), each link keeping its length. (The squid's arms and the ray's wings, and the code for being dragged down, are gone.) |
| 506 | Creatures still look messed up sometimes, like floating body parts | The tusks (FIX 494) rose out of the middle of the lower jaw straight up, and on a long face they went up in front of it clear of it, hanging there. They come out of the side of the jaw now, sunk into it, shorter, sweeping out and up past the cheek. |
| 507 | Giants' heads need redoing -- weird, clipping into the body, too far inside it; the back can look wrong; the tentacles don't connect fully -- make them feel alive, actually grab the player, long enough for the giant's height; it tries to stab you even when you're too far; its legs clip into facilities; it should bend down to look at you or through windows; it should ragdoll dying | The head hangs out in front of the hull now on a neck grown out of the hull's front, not half sunk in it, and can turn no further round than its neck lets it -- turned to look behind, it had turned back into its own body. The spines on its back were finer at their tips than the hull's grid could hold and came out as broken triangles; they are thicker there. The strands grow out of its underside on the skin and are thick where they join it, long enough to reach the ground (as long as it is tall), never quite still -- each sways and curls slowly of its own -- and they are what it takes somebody with: one reaches down for them, whips round them and lifts them to its eyes (a strider with none reaches down with a leg). It reaches only for somebody within its reach -- it stabbed at the ground from too far away. Any building is in its way now, as far out as its feet stand round it, and it is pushed off one its feet would come down round: stepping over buildings, its legs went through them. Close to somebody, or where they went in out of its sight, it bends right down on its legs to look at them -- or in at the windows. And dead, its legs give and their feet slide out, and its body comes down under its own weight onto the ground with a bounce and lies there, its head down -- instead of being tipped over onto its side whole. |
