And the cloud deck was never lost in the fog with the distance, as the land is: in a downpour the far deck stayed dark behind trees fogged pale, their outlines cut out against it. It is now, by the scene's own fog. |
| 649 | You can get rid of these paths or make them a different texture, and trees and rocks shouldn't be able to generate on them | The trails worn from the landing to the doors are now packed earth with grit in it -- browner and darker than the ground either side, reading as a path -- and nothing is put on one or on a road any more: no tree, rock, bush or tuft, and the land grows thinner near them. |
| 650 | The giants walking on buildings still look weird | Its feet went up onto roofs and down again, its body heaving up and down over every building it crossed. Its feet stay on the ground now: it straddles a building, its feet either side, or goes round one it cannot. |
| 651 | It sometimes looks like it has all legs off the ground at once | A foot fallen far enough behind was lifted whatever the others were doing -- with the rest already up, every leg was in the air. Two feet at least are always on the ground now. |
| 652 | Its attack doesn't feel very impactful | Its blow coming down -- the stamp, the strand whipped onto the ground -- did nothing but to whoever it caught. Now, on every machine as the blow lands: the ground jumps hard under it and round it (felt over its own size), it is heard as a great weight coming down, every creature about hears it, and anybody within nine metres is thrown back off their feet whether it had them or not. |
| 653 | Sometimes I can stare at it and nothing happens, like it doesn't see me, when it's waiting next to a door | Lying in wait, it faced the doorway and nothing else, and nothing in its waiting asked whether anybody was in front of it. Now it turns to look about now and then -- a way to either side, low, and back -- and found where it waits it does not go on waiting: somebody right on top of it it springs at; somebody looking straight at it from near by, the bold go for them and the rest slip away. (A lab test: found two seconds into its wait, eight metres off to one side and stared at, it had slipped away within three.) |
| 654 | The AI doesn't handle airlocks great | Asked to open, an airlock cycles for a few seconds before its door opens -- and a creature took that for a door that would not open: it pulled at it again (which could undo the cycle) and gave it up after three goes. It knows an airlock is cycling now and waits for it in front of the door, as anybody does, and pulling at one already cycling does nothing. |
| 655 | The creatures' faces: whatever fits the lore best, and more variation -- faces that open sideways or at several points | A new kind of face: the snout made of three to five petals closed round a mouth down its middle, each on its own bone, deep slits between them, rows of teeth down the inside of each and wet flesh within; the rig peels them back from the middle as it opens its mouth -- parted as it breathes hard, flung wide to bite or roar. Crawlers have a person's skull far less often now (a fifth, from three in five): a third have the petals; other kinds have them about one time in five. (Not yet looked at in the game.) |
| 656 | When I press F3 in game, the game crashes (loading a solo run, in the ship, sometimes in game) | The Mission window the F3 overlay draws named the mission's kind and the device up close from lists that had not been given the newer ones: a solo run with no contract (an explore) and the keypad, the note, the fuse box and the cabinet all read off the end of their list, and the game went down. Every name is there now, and a name is looked up only if it is one. |
| 657 | The water droplets on my camera should not show up in cutscenes | The drops on the visor (from coming out of water, or rain) are now never drawn while a cutscene is filming: that camera is not your visor. |
| 658 | When landing I can see the edge of the world, and I didn't see the sand load in until I got close | The land round a site reaches twelve kilometres; past it is the globe, drawn under it. Coming down, the land faded at its rim into one colour and the globe round it was lit over again into another -- the land stood as a square on a plain of a different shade. The globe round the site now takes the very colour the land fades into, and so does the sky below the horizon, so the land runs on into the globe with no edge. The fade itself now comes on whenever you are high over the land -- the ship coming down on its own, a camera flown up -- not only in the shuttle's dive, eased in with the height, and it fades into the colour the land round the site is actually painted (a jungle's green), not its bare ground's (a sandy beige). |
| 659 | When it's foggy and you are underwater you can see the fog underwater fully | Two things carried the fog down into the water: the water's murk took in some of the air's fog colour (a foggy day's pale grey all through it), and the mist lying in the hollows was drawn over everything with the eye under it -- under water too. Neither now: under water the fog only dims the day coming down through it. |
| 660 | When entering the planet when there is fog it looks weird and wrong a lot of the time | Coming down, the fog was put out further the higher the eye -- the land a grey sheet, then all at once clear with pale hard-edged patches of mist along every contour, then lost in the fog again. Now a fog is a bank lying on the land, about a hundred metres deep and thinning above: from high up its top is seen with hills standing out of it, and it closes in steadily as you come down into it. The mist in the hollows is only as thick as the way through it -- a metre or two of it seen from above is hardly there. And a thin dark line along the horizon past the land (the sky just under the horizon left its own colour) is gone. |
| 661 | The ice texture needs to be redone to look realistic | Redrawn as a frozen lake's ice: clear and dark over deep water (the black of the water through it, milky in streaks), frosted white where it is thin at the shore, snow blown over it in drifts with bare swept ice between, cracked across in a web of jagged plates large and small (the cracks white in it), bubbles caught in the clear, and the clear ice giving back the sky smoothly while the frost and snow are matt. Everything is warped off the world's grid, so the old blotchy tile pattern is gone at every height, and the cracks fade out rather than shimmer far off. |
| 662 | (Found testing the ice) On a frozen site the creatures never got their way about, and the game hung when quitting | The frozen sea was something to stand on all the way out to the horizon, and the creatures' navigation, built over everything that can be stood on, was set to cover twenty-four kilometres square: it never finished, so nothing could find a path, and quitting waited for it forever. The ice is solid only as far as anybody can go (a margin past it), and the navigation now leaves out anything far outside the site or hundreds of metres across. It builds in under a second there now, and the game closes. |
| 663 | The teeth don't connect and the mouth looks weird still, can you rework this | The petalled face is rebuilt. The petals were thin spikes sticking out of the snout, and the head's own jaw still dropped under them like a second mouth. Now each petal is a thick, broad curved shell; shut, they lie edge to edge as one closed bud with fine slits between, tapering to a point, a ridge down each one's back. Inside is a wet red hollow, and each petal carries two staggered rows of teeth rooted in its flesh that point in across the hollow, so shut the points of all the petals meet in the middle. The jaw no longer drops on a petalled head; the petals open instead, no further than square to the snout (they used to fold back over the head). |
| 664 | Can you make their eyes still glow but look more realistic | Each eye is now a wet, dark, glossy eyeball sitting a little back in its socket, catching the light. The glow is on its front as an animal's eyes shine back: a vertical slit bright down its middle and fading out across it, in a dim ring of iris. It used to be a flat orange dot down in the dark of the socket. |
| 665 | The leviathan misses its attack more than anything, it keeps going "past them" -- maybe as I was on the roof | It rushed at anybody it saw, however high over the water they stood: its jaws closed on the air under somebody up on a roof or a high deck, and it carried on past them, again and again. It now goes for nobody it cannot reach -- higher over the water than its jaws come up at the top of its rush, or on land away from the water. Out of its reach it stays under them, circling slowly near the top, and goes for them once they come down to the water. (A test: twelve metres up over the sea, never rushed in thirty seconds; down in the water, gone for.) |
| 666 | Whatever this face is, I really don't like it -- it just looks like it has a big chin | The petal face (FIX 663) made a bulging bud slung under the face. It is replaced (you left the pick to me) by mandibles: two long curved jaws either side of the snout, level with the middle of the face, narrowing to hooked points that cross when shut, a ridge down each one's back. They open sideways, and two staggered rows of teeth down each one's inside interlock with the other's in the middle. The head is no deeper than any other head now, so nothing hangs under the face. |
| 667 | Can you make the outside of the planets look 3D and better so it looks better when getting close to it and landing | Up close a world's surface was its maps and nothing finer: from a low orbit and through the dive, a flat smeared painted skin. Now, as it comes closer, ranges, hills and ridges come in at three sizes, each only once it is big enough on the screen, their sides lit and shaded by the sun, crests paler and low ground darker. It is regional, not even all over: rugged high country with sharp ridged crests in some places, gentler country and plains between, bare rock paler and greyer. |
| 668 | Still the landing spot glows blue when you are landing | Round the place being come down to, the globe was tinted towards a colour worked out from the haze, which shifted its hue: a teal glow on the land. It now keeps the globe's own colours there and only matches how light or dark the land is. |
| 669 | If there is destruction we don't need an airlock, because it would have been destroyed | Half-fallen buildings already had none; burnt ones, broken open by the fire, had them still. No destroyed building has an airlock now (on the sea's floor a building keeps its lockout, as it is the only way in). |
| 670 | In the terrain there is a gap through the world (by a ruined building) | Two openings at a ruin's broken corner: the broken floor was a bare plane, so where the land outside fell away under its edge there was a black gap under it; and the ground was cut over any stairwell through the ground floor even where that part of the building had come down, leaving a pit into the dark. The ruin's floor now has its slab and footing down into the ground along every open edge, and the ground is cut over stairs only where the building still stands. |
| 671 | Make the giants and leviathans have the same eyes as the new creatures | Their eyes are now the creatures' (FIX 664): wet dark glossy balls, the glow on their fronts a slit bright down its middle fading across it in a dim ring of iris, nothing round the back. They used to be flat glowing beads. The strider's look out ahead, the leviathan's out to the side. |
| 672 | You are able to swim through the leviathan; fix this so it has collision | Only rounds could find its body; nothing stopped a person. Down its spine it now has a solid body along each length, a little inside what rounds find, following it as it swims: swum into, it stops you, and it shoves you aside coming through. |
| 673 | When the leviathan dies it turns on its back way too quickly, and it doesn't collide with buildings | It now begins turning over a few seconds after it dies and takes most of a minute, as the air in it lifts its belly (it went over in a couple of seconds). And dead, its body is kept out of what is built: lying on a roof it comes down on, stopped against a wall it drifts into. |
| 674 | When the giant dies it's way too stiff; and the tentacles need to die after a bit when it dies | Its legs stayed jacked up into the air with their knees high. Now its knees give, falling outwards and down, and its feet slide out until the legs lie nearly flat. It comes down heavier, slumping further onto its side, rolling on a little past where it settles and back; its head hangs. Its strands lose their hold on their shape over a few seconds after it dies and lie limp on the ground. |
| 675 | When swimming in and out of airlocks there is a sudden lighting change, you can see clearly in the water for a second, and you can't see anything out of the door very clearly | An airlock on the sea's floor lets the sea itself in, but its water was counted as a building's stale flood: a few metres' murk lit only by what came in, against the open sea's tens of metres lit by the day from above; and its doorway, between the two, counted as neither for a moment -- clear air. In an airlock with the sea in it, or in its doorway, it is now the sea itself: as murky and as lit as just outside, the same all the way through, so you see out of its door as far as you would out there. |
| 676 | (Phase 7) Wildlife -- "so far I don't like the wildlife; they should look like real animals, not the creatures: birds and fish and stuff like that, Earth-like" | The creature-built grazers and runners you saw were dropped. In their place, a wildlife system of its own with real animal shapes: flocks of birds over the land (crows, small birds in tight quick flocks low down, a hawk or two circling high, gulls over the sea and the ice), wings beating and gliding as each kind does, banking round their circles; schools of fish in open water round you, bodies and tails swishing, fins on their backs, darting off from you when you swim close. Birds are out by day and roost at night and in storms; a shot near a flock puts it up and away, and it drifts back over half a minute. Most worlds with air have some birds (airless ones none); there are fish wherever the water is liquid (not ice, lava or acid). They follow the site's seed and the campaign's clock, so every player sees them in the same places. Dev command: wildlife_view [flock] [metres]. |
| 677 | When the leviathan dies it was still playing sounds, and its head got twisted, turned sharp | Dead, it no longer calls in the water. And its body as it floats is no longer a chain with nothing stiff in it: each joint is kept in line with its neighbours, its head with its neck, so it lies in a gentle curve and its head can't fold round sharply. |
| 678 | The giants' face looks squished sometimes; get rid of the head that looks like this (eyes out on stalks); the giants should be able to have the same heads as the creatures | The hammerhead (eyes out on lobes) and the flat shovel head are gone; the blunt head is longer in the face. In their place, two of the creatures' kinds of face: a maw -- a deep heavy jaw under a long low mouth, its teeth long -- and mandibles, two curved jaws out at the front of the muzzle hooking in to points, opening with its jaw. |
| 679 | When it was turning, one of its legs rotated through its body | Turning on the spot, its hips went round with its body while a planted foot stayed where it was, until the leg crossed under it. Now a foot the body has turned more than 40 degrees away from is stepped round at once. |
| 680 | The giants still try to place their legs weirdly sometimes; most of the giants' legs clipped into the ground | A knee can no longer go into the ground: over a rise between hip and foot it's lifted clear and the leg bent higher. Dead, the knees now fall outwards and a little up rather than down into the ground. |
| 681 | The tips of the tentacles aren't there | The strands' tubes stopped at a quarter of their thickness, open at the end, so the tips looked cut off. They now taper to a point. |
| 682 | (Phase 7) The signs reworked: tracks, drag marks, feeding remains, scratched doors, shed skin -- how long ago and how big | What lives at a site now leaves marks besides the organism's formations. Its tracks run along the ways it goes, from its lair down to the nearest water and out to the buildings' doors, pressed into the ground in mud or snow: a four-toed pad with claw marks, a crawler's long-fingered hands, a biped's three-toed prints, a giant strider's pits metres across. Near its lair are the remains of what it ate (a stain on the ground, a row of backbone, ribs, long bones, a skull), with a drag mark smeared along the ground to them. A building it lives in has claw gouges through the paint beside its doors, and a pale crumpled shed skin lies by its lair. Fresh marks (it's about now) are dark, crisp and many; old ones (it only came through a while back) are faded and fewer; their size is its size. Nothing where nothing lives, or where it lives in the sea. |
| 683 | (Phase 7) Creatures riding lifts | Every lift is now a way between each pair of its stops for any creature that fits through a door. One whose way goes by a lift walks to its doors and calls it (pressing now and then, as anybody waiting does), steps in when the doors open, sends the car to its floor, rides it up or down, and steps out when the doors open there. It goes through the lift's own rules, the same as players: on every machine, never shut in. If the lift has no power or it's kept waiting a minute, it gives lifts up for a while and goes another way. (Feet on stairs and slopes, weight shifting and a gait for each size were already in: the body tilts to the slope, sinks as each foot takes it, sways its weight side to side, and steps on stairs at their real height.) |
| 684 | (Phase 7) Kinds fighting each other: a big one hunting a small one, rivals over ground | Where something lives, now and then (a quarter of the time, more further out) another kind has come through nearby: a big one where what lives there is small, a small pack where it's big. When they're near each other and nobody is close by, the bigger one scents the other from up to 140 m and runs it down, biting it when it catches it (real damage, heard); the smaller runs once it's within 30 m. Both leave it as soon as people are within 12 m: they're what they're after. A test: a big visitor found a small resident feeding at its lair and killed it in eight bites. Dev: site_fauna ... rivals / norivals. |
| 685 | (Phase 7) Small ones in numbers (vent-sized swarms) and many-legged runners | Tiny creatures now sometimes come as a swarm of six to nine (otherwise three or four, or one alone). And about a third of six-legged builds -- the long and low ones, and now and then any -- are many-legged runners: four to seven pairs of thinner legs under a body drawn out half again as long, quicker than anything their size. Both are worked out from the seed alone, so no existing creature changed. |
| 686 | (Phase 6) Surveys set up by hand -- "the markers come in a crate in the shuttle's hold: each carried out to its place, planted, its legs kicked out, levelled with its bubble, switched on; read once three are up; a marker knocked over stops reading until set up again; at the end left (cheaper) or packed up and carried home (pays more)" | A survey no longer has markers standing ready to stand next to. Its markers lie in a crate strapped on the shuttle cabin's right-hand bench, one for each survey point, picked up like anything else and carried upright in the hand. Each survey point is an orange flag on a pin: pressed with a marker in hand, the marker is pushed into the ground there (without one, it says where they are). Pressed again, it comes up close, worked with the pointer: its three legs are kicked out (each clicked; clicked again folds it), each leg's clamp lets it out a step (the longest back to the shortest), and the switch on its head turns it on. The ground under each foot is a little uneven (different every time), so it leans until each leg is let out by as much as the ground falls away under it: the bubble on its head sits to one side until it is in its ring. Its lamp blinks amber while it is on but cannot read (legs, level, knocked over), pulses blue while it waits for others, blinks bright while reading and goes green once its reading is taken. Readings only come in while at least three markers are working at once (they triangulate), each taking forty seconds; working markers ping every few seconds, heard by whatever listens, so something may come to see. Anything that walks into a standing marker -- a creature, or a giant's foot -- knocks it over with a clatter: it lies there, its legs folded, until it is stood back up, its legs kicked out and levelled again (the ground under it is never quite as it was). Once its reading is taken, a marker switched off and folded can be pulled up and is laid beside where it stood, to be carried back aboard. Each marker brought back aboard (carried, or lying in the shuttle) pays 30 credits on top (contracts.json marker_reward), shown on the debrief; left behind, nothing. The objective now says to set up a marker at each point, how many are working and how far the readings have got, and (optional) how many markers are aboard. Everybody sees the same markers in multiplayer (sent as a survey message), and a player's own marker leaves their hands as they plant it. (Test commands: survey_set n/all, survey_set knock n, mission_closeup marker n, mission_work marker n control.) |
| 687 | Can you rework the creatures' eyes again and make more of it glow | The glow now covers nearly the whole front of each eye, a domed lens over the dark ball, not just a slit down its middle: the iris lit all over and streaked with fibres out from the middle, hottest in a ring round the pupil, darker towards its rim, the pupil a dark upright slit. The same on creatures, giants and leviathans, made finer (more of the eye's surface) so the streaks show. |
| 688 | The giant's foot planting could still be better: should the body go up when it goes on a building? And the legs still lag behind sometimes (picture: over a roof, its feet left hanging in the air) | Its feet were put down wherever was clear of buildings, however far that was -- over a broad building, out of reach of its legs, which then stretched towards them and stopped short in the air -- while its body was held up over the roof by nothing. Now a foot is only ever put down where its leg reaches from its hip; while there is ground in reach it stays off anything built, and where there is none (over a broad building) it goes down well in on the roof, and its body comes up with its feet. Its body is never held higher than its legs reach from where its feet are. A step is no longer lifted over something taller than it lifts a foot (a mast, a tower: the leg went straight up over its body) -- that foot goes down short of it or past its side. And it waits for its feet: with one well behind where it would go, it slows (eased, so it doesn't lurch) until the foot is across. A strike's whip now gathers over its first moments rather than starting at full speed. |
| 689 | Leviathan death: I want its body to bend freely. And the giants' legs still go into the ground and clip through buildings -- they should both pretty much just ragdoll | Both are now real ragdolls in the physics. A dead strider's hull and each of its legs' two lengths are solid pieces joined loosely at the hips and knees, let go as they were the moment it died: it folds and comes down under its own weight onto the ground, and onto and against whatever is built, never into it -- its legs lying spread however they land, its head and jaw going with its hull. A dead leviathan is its spine link by link, each joined to the next and free to bend a little at every joint, so the whole of it curves however it lies; the water buoys it up (an armoured one sinks) and it comes to rest along the surface or on the sea floor, against what is built rather than through it. The old made-up death animations (the slump onto its side, feet sliding out, the leviathan's slow roll onto its back) are gone. Each machine runs its own, as with creatures. |
